Deformations of ideals in Lie algebras Thanks: This project was partially funded by the RTG 2491 in Göttingen and by a GSSP-DAAD fellowship at the University of Göttingen. Thanks: ∗ Corresponding author
Ilias Ermeidis ∗,⋆,1, Madeleine Jotz †

Abstract
. This paper develops the deformation theory of Lie ideals. It shows that the smooth deformations of an ideal 𝔦 \mathfrak{i} in a Lie algebra 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} differentiate to cohomology classes in the cohomology of 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} with values in its adjoint representation on Hom ⁡ ( 𝔦 , 𝔤 / 𝔦 ) \Hom(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}) . The cohomology associated with the ideal 𝔦 \mathfrak{i} in 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} is compared with other Lie algebra cohomologies defined by 𝔦 \mathfrak{i} , such as the cohomology defined by 𝔦 \mathfrak{i} as a Lie subalgebra of 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} , and the cohomology defined by the Lie algebra morphism 𝔤 → 𝔤 / 𝔦 \mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i} . After a choice of complement of the ideal 𝔦 \mathfrak{i} in the Lie algebra 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} , its deformation complex is enriched to the differential graded Lie algebra that controls its deformations, in the sense that its Maurer-Cartan elements are in one-to-one correspondence with the (small) deformations of the ideal. These constructions are shown to hold independently of the choice of complement – up to isomorphism. Furthermore, the L ∞ L_{\infty} -algebra that simultaneously controls the deformations of 𝔦 \mathfrak{i} and of the ambient Lie bracket is identified. Under appropriate assumptions on the low degrees of the deformation cohomology of a given Lie ideal, the (topological) rigidity and stability of ideals are studied, as well as obstructions to deformations of ideals of Lie algebras.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary:
17B56, Secondary:
14D15

1.
Introduction

Ideals in Lie algebras are crucial in the representation theory and classification of the latter.
Therefore, for understanding a given Lie algebra, it is crucial to have a good
comprehension of its Lie ideals — such as how many there are, i.e. whether there are none, few,
or many, and how much these essentially differ from one another.
This is where
deformation theory comes into play.
Deformation theory serves as a powerful tool for understanding how mathematical structures change under formal or smooth perturbations. Originating in algebraic geometry, where it was used to study the deformations of complex structures, the theory has since found wide-ranging applications in algebra, topology, and mathematical physics. In the context of Lie algebras, deformation theory investigates how the structure of a Lie algebra can be modified or preserved under small changes to its Lie bracket.

The foundations of deformation theory
were
laid by Kodaira and Spencer in their remarkable series of works on deformations of
complex manifolds
[
21
,
20
,
22
,
23
]
.
Gerstenhaber then studied deformations of rings
and associative algebras
[
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
11
]
. Foundational work in this area was conducted as well in the 1960’s by Nijenhuis and Richardson, who developed key techniques for studying infinitesimal deformations of algebraic structures. In particular, they introduced the concept of deformation cohomology and provided criteria for the integrability of infinitesimal deformations into formal deformations. Their work established that the deformation theory of Lie algebras can be elegantly formulated using the language of differential graded Lie algebras (dgLa), which naturally encode both the deformation problem and its obstructions, see
[
31
,
32
,
36
,
35
,
34
,
33
,
39
,
41
,
37
,
40
]
. Despite these advancements, surprisingly enough, the deformation theory of Lie ideals seems to be, so far, almost totally
absent in the literature.
While the deformation theory of Lie subalgebras has been partially addressed in certain contexts, the specific case of ideals — a central and invariant feature of Lie algebras —remains largely unexplored. This gap in the literature is striking, given that the behavior of ideals under deformations can influence both the internal structure of the algebra and its external representations.

This paper addresses this gap by associating to any ideal in a Lie algebra a dgLa that controls its deformations. Furthermore, it introduces an
L_{\infty}
-algebra that governs the simultaneous deformations of the Lie bracket in the ambient Lie algebra and the ideal itself. These constructions provide a new perspective on the deformation theory of Lie algebras, with significant implications for understanding rigidity, stability, and obstruction phenomena.

A well-known principle in deformation theory states, roughly speaking, that behind
every reasonable deformation problem of a mathematical structure of a specific type,
there is a differential graded Lie algebra (dgLa for short) or, more generally, an
L_{\infty}
-algebra,
which “controls” the deformation problem – in the sense that its Maurer-Cartan elements are in bijective correspondence with the deformations of the initial structure. This
philosophy can be traced back to Deligne, Drinfeld, Kontsevich and many others
[
6
,
7
,
17
,
24
,
18
,
29
]
. Around fifteen years ago, Lurie
[
28
]
and
Pridham
[
38
]
formalized this heuristic philosophy of deformation theory into an equivalence between formal moduli problems and differential graded Lie algebras in characteristic
zero, using higher category theory.

Returning to the main focus of this paper; given a Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}
and a Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
, the following question arises where the adjective “controlling” is
meant in the aforementioned sense.

Question 1: What is the controlling differential graded Lie algebra of the deformation problem of a Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
?

This paper answers the above question by describing explicitly the deformation cochain complex
together with its graded Lie algebra structure and proving that it controls the “small” deformations of the Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
.
Moduli theory deals with the study of the geometry of moduli spaces, which are spaces
whose points represent equivalence classes of algebro-geometric objects. To the best of the
knowledge of the authors, the prototypical and perhaps the most elegant example of a moduli space is
the (real) Grassmannian of a vector space. Given an
n
-dimensional vector space
V
, the set of isomorphism
classes of
k
-dimensional vector subspaces, denoted
\Gr_{k}(V)
, carries a natural smooth manifold structure of dimension equal to
k(n-k)
and is called the
k
-Grassmannian of
V
. Except
for this beautiful example, in almost all other cases it is difficult to understand (at least immediately) the global geometry of a moduli space, due to the presence of singularities and/or of
infinite dimensionality. Hence the infinitesimal nature of a moduli space must first
and foremost be understood.

Some of the questions in deformation theory
concern when a mathematical structure of interest is rigid or stable under deformations. Roughly speaking, rigidity questions are related to identifying isolated points of the
moduli space of the studied structure, while stability questions concern its local smoothness around the distinguished point.
Given a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, the space
\mathbf{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
of
k
-dimensional Lie ideals is a
subspace of
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
. A Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
is
rigid under the natural action of
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
on
\mathbf{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})

if the space of
k
-dimensional Lie ideals
\mathbf{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
coincides locally, in some open neighborhood
of
\mathfrak{i}\in\mathbf{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\subseteq\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
, with the
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
-orbit of
\mathfrak{i}
.

Question 2: Under what assumption is a Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}

\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
-rigid?

An ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
in a Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
is called
stable
if for any Lie bracket
\mu^{\prime}
on
\mathfrak{g}
sufficiently close to
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
,
there exists a Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}\lhd(\mathfrak{g},\mu^{\prime})
sufficiently close to
\mathfrak{i}\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})

Question 3: Under what assumption is a Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
stable?

This paper provides sufficient criteria for both the rigidity and
stability of a Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}
in a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
By developing deformation cohomologies tailored to ideals, the authors not only establish theoretical foundations but also explore geometric applications, including the use of the Kuranishi map to study obstructions to deformations of ideals in Lie algebras. This work thus represents a novel contribution to the broader landscape of deformation theory in the context of Lie algebras and opens up new avenues for the long-standing, active research on their classification theory.

Outline of the paper

Section
2

introduces the mathematical framework necessary for the studies in this paper, including graded vector spaces, differential graded Lie algebras, and
L_{\infty}
-algebras. These structures form the algebraic foundation for the deformation theories discussed here.
Then standard deformation complexes associated with Lie algebras are reviewed, such as the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex, and the notation and conventions used throughout the paper are set up.
Section
2.3
in particular
demonstrates how Lie algebras and Lie algebras with representations can be understood as Maurer-Cartan elements of special differential graded Lie algebras. This establishes the framework for associating dgLa structures to deformations.

Section
3

recalls existing results on the deformation theory of Lie subalgebras, including their description as Maurer-Cartan elements in dgLa frameworks. It lays the groundwork for extending these ideas to the case of Lie ideals.
Section
4
then defines smooth and infinitesimal deformations of ideals in Lie algebras. It provides examples and clarifies their distinctions from subalgebra deformations.
Then it explores the connections between the deformation cohomology of an ideal and those for the same ideal as a subalgebra, as well as those of morphisms associated to ideals in Lie algebras, highlighting novel differences and insights.

Section
5

constructs a Voronov dataset associated to an ideal in a Lie algebra and uses it to define the unique, up to isomorphism, dgL[1]a that controls the deformations of the ideal. It then develops an
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra that governs the simultaneous deformations of the Lie bracket and the ideal, providing a unified framework for understanding these processes.

Section
6
introduces the Kuranishi map as a tool for identifying obstructions to deformations. It discusses its role in understanding when deformations can be extended or are blocked,
and it examines then (cohomological) conditions under which ideals remain rigid under perturbations, before also establishing criteria for their stability.

Appendix
A
provides necessary supplementary material on Grassmannian manifolds for the considerations in this paper.

2.
Preliminaries

This section collects necessary preliminaries for the contents of this paper.

2.1.
Graded vector spaces and L
∞
-algebras

L
∞
-algebras are used to describe deformations of algebraic structures. Their definition and properties are summarized in this section.

A
graded vector space

\mathbf{V}
is the
direct sum of a family of vector spaces
(V_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{Z})
, that comes equipped as follows with a grading.
An element
v\in\mathbf{V}
is called
degree-homogeneous
if
v\in V_{i}
for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and the
degree
of
v
is then defined to be
|v|=i
. That is, for

i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the degree
i
component of
\mathbf{V}
(denoted with lower
index
\mathbf{V}_{i}
) equals
V_{i}
. The component
V_{i}
of
\mathbf{V}
is then written
V_{i}[-i]
for recording its degree, i.e.

\mathbf{V}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}[-i].

That is,
V_{i}
, which as a classical vector space has elements of degree
0
, is shifted by
-i
in the following sense.
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the
degree
k
-shift

\mathbf{V}[k]
of
\mathbf{V}
is the graded vector space

\mathbf{V}[k]=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}[-i+k]=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{j+k}[-j],

i.e. with

(\mathbf{V}[k])_{j}=V_{j+k}
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Unless specified otherwise, the vector space
\mathbf{V}
has finite dimension. That is, only finitely many of its summands are non-trivial and have then finite dimension.

The usual constructions with vector spaces can be similarly done in the graded setting.
Let
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{W}
be two graded vector spaces.

(1)

The direct sum
\mathbf{V}\oplus\mathbf{W}
is given by
(\mathbf{V}\oplus\mathbf{W})_{i}=V_{i}\oplus W_{i}
for all
i
, i.e.

\mathbf{V}\oplus\mathbf{W}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(V_{i}+W_{i})[-i].

(2)

The tensor product
\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{W}
is graded by
(\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{W})_{i}=\oplus_{j+k=i}\ V_{j}\otimes W_{k}
, i.e.

\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{W}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\bigoplus_{j+k=i}V_{j}\otimes W_{k}\right)[-i].

(3)

The dual
\mathbf{V}^{*}
of
\mathbf{V}
is the graded vector space

\mathbf{V}^{*}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}^{*}[i].

(4)

The graded vector space
\textbf{Hom}(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W})\simeq\mathbf{V}^{*}\otimes\mathbf{W}
is then defined by

\textbf{Hom}(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W})=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\oplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Hom(V_{j},W_{i+j})\right)[-i]

and, as usual, denoted by
\textbf{End}(\mathbf{V})\simeq\mathbf{V}^{*}\otimes\mathbf{V}
when
\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{W}
.

In particular a (degree 0)
linear map

f\colon\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{W}
between graded vector spaces is a collection of degree-preserving linear maps
\{f_{i}\colon V_{i}\to W_{i}\}
. A
degree
k
linear map
from
V
to
W
is a linear map
f\colon\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{W}[k]
i.e.
f
is a collection of linear maps
f_{i}\colon V_{i}\to W_{i+k}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
(with, by definition, all but finitely many of these maps being
0
).

(5)

The tensor algebra
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{V})=\oplus_{l\geq 0}\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}
is graded by the total degree

|v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{l}|=|v_{1}|+\cdots|v_{l}|

since according to the considerations above

(\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{V}))_{i}=\oplus_{l\geq 0}(\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l})_{i}=\oplus_{l\geq 0}\oplus_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{l}=i}V_{i_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{i_{l}}

for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Here, by convention
\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 0}=\mathbb{R}
has degree
0
.
The
graded symmetric algebra

\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{V})
of
\mathbf{V}
is the quotient of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{\mathbf{V}})
by the two-sided ideal generated by elements of the form
x\otimes y-(-1)^{|x||y|}y\otimes x
. Similarly, the
graded exterior algebra

{\bigwedge}(\mathbf{V})
of
\mathbf{V}
is the quotient of
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{V})
by the two-sided ideal generated by elements of the form
x\otimes y+(-1)^{|x||y|}y\otimes x
.

Remark 2.1
.

(1)

The graded vector spaces
\mathbf{T}(\mathbf{V})
,
{\bigwedge}(\mathbf{V})
and
\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{V})
do not have finite dimension in general.

(2)

The graded tensor algebra in the last construction is in fact bigraded, since an element

x\in V_{i_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{i_{l}}
has as well the
polynomial degree

l
. Its bidegree is hence
(l,i_{1}+\ldots+i_{l})
.
Precisely, the elements of
\mathbf{T}^{k}\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
have polynomial degree
k
.
Analogously, the elements of
\mathbf{S}^{k}(\mathbf{V})
and
\bigwedge^{k}(\mathbf{V})
have polynomial degree
k
.

A
graded Lie algebra

(\mathbf{V},[\cdot,\cdot])
is a graded vector space
\mathbf{V}
equipped with a
\mathbb{R}
-bilinear bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]\colon\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

the bracket is degree-preserving:
[V_{i},V_{j}]\subset V_{i+j}
for
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
,

and

(2)

the bracket is
graded skew-symmetric
:
[x,y]=-(-1)^{|x||y|}[y,x]
for all degree-homogeneous elements
x,y\in\mathbf{V}
,

(3)

the bracket satisfies the
graded Jacobi identity

[x,[y,z]]=[[x,y],z]+(-1)^{|x||y|}[y,[x,z]]

for degree-homogeneous elements
x,y,z\in\mathbf{V}
.

A
differential graded Lie algebra (dgLa)
is then a triple
(\mathbf{V},[\cdot,\cdot],\mathbf{d})
where
(\mathbf{V},[\cdot,\cdot])
is a graded Lie algebra and
\mathbf{d}\colon\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}
is a degree 1 linear map such that

(1)

\mathbf{d}[x,y]=[\mathbf{d}(x),y]+(-1)^{|x|}[x,\mathbf{d}(y)]
(that is,
\mathbf{d}
is a degree
1
derivation with respect to the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
)

(2)

\mathbf{d}^{2}=0
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leq i\leq n
a permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
is called an
(i,n-i)
-unshuffle
if it satisfies
\sigma(1)<\cdots<\sigma(i)
and
\sigma(i+1)<\cdots<\sigma(n).
The set of
(i,n-i)
-unshuffles is denoted by
S_{(i,n-i)}
.

Consider a graded vector space
\mathbf{V}
as above and two of its degree homogeneous elements
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. Then as elements of
\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{V})
,

x_{1}
and
x_{2}
satisfy

x_{1}\cdot x_{2}=(-1)^{|x_{1}|\cdot|x_{2}|}x_{2}\cdot x_{1}=:\epsilon((12);x_{1},x_{2})\cdot x_{2}\cdot x_{1}

i.e. with
\epsilon((12);x_{1},x_{2})\in\{-1,1\}
defined by this equation.
More generally for
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathbf{V}
degree-homogeneous, the
Koszul sign

\epsilon(\sigma;x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\{-1,1\}
of a permutation
\sigma
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
is defined by

x_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{n}=\epsilon(\sigma;x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\cdot x_{\sigma(1)}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{\sigma(n)}.

Definition 2.2
.

(1)

An
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra
is a graded vector space
\mathbf{V}
equipped with a
collection

\left\{m_{k}\colon S^{k}\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}[1]\right\}_{k\geq 1}

of linear maps, satisfying the following relations for all homogeneous elements
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathbf{V}
:

(1)

\sum_{i+j=n+1}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{(i,n-i)}}\epsilon(\sigma;x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})m_{j}(m_{i}(x_{\sigma(1)},\dots,x_{\sigma(i)}),x_{\sigma(i+1)},\dots,x_{\sigma(n)})=0.

(2)

A
dgL
[1]
a
is an
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra
(\mathbf{V},m_{1},m_{2})
, i.e. with
m_{k}=0
for all
k\geq 3
.

(3)

The notion of
curved
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra
generalizes the notion of
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra by allowing the presence of an element
m_{0}\in V_{1}[-1]
, and so permitting the indices
i,j
in (
1
) to take the value zero. Hence, an extra relation arises, corresponding to the case
n=0
in (
1
).

Definition 2.3
.

(1)

A
Maurer-Cartan element
of a dgLa
(\mathbf{V},[\cdot,\cdot],\mathbf{d})
is an element
x\in\mathbf{V}
of degree
1
satisfying the following equation:

(2)

\mathbf{d}(x)+\frac{1}{2}[x,x]=0.

This equation (
2
) is called the
Maurer-Cartan equation
.

(2)

More generally, a
Maurer-Cartan element
of a
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra
(\mathbf{V},m_{1},m_{2},\ldots,m_{l})
with finitely many multibrackets is an element
x
of degree
0
in
\mathbf{V}
such that

(3)

\sum_{k=1}^{l}\frac{1}{k!}m_{k}(x,\ldots,x)=0.

If
\big(\mathbf{V},m_{0},m_{1},\dots,m_{l}\big)
is a curved
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra, then the above sum starts from
k=0
.

Definition 2.4
.

[
43
,
3
,
30
,
25
]

Let
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{W}
be two graded vector spaces equipped with

L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra structures
(m_{k})_{k\geq 1}
and

(n_{k})_{k\geq 1}
respectively.

A
morphism of
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebras
from

(\mathbf{V},(m_{k})_{k\geq 1})

to

(\mathbf{W},(n_{k})_{k\geq 1})

is a family of morphisms

F:=\bigl(f_{k}\colon S^{k}\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{W}\bigr)_{k\geq 1}

of graded vector spaces,
such that the two families of morphisms

S^{k}\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{W}[1]

given by

\sum_{r+s=k}\sum_{\tau\in S_{(r,s)}}\epsilon(\tau;x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})f_{s+1}\Bigl(m_{r}(x_{\tau(1)},\cdots,x_{\tau(r)}),x_{\tau(r+1)},\cdots,x_{\tau(k)}\Bigr)

and

\sum_{l=1}^{k}\sum_{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{l}=k}\sum_{\tau\in S_{k}}\frac{\epsilon(\tau;x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})}{l!\,j_{1}!\cdots j_{l}!}\,n_{l}\Bigl(f_{j_{1}}(x_{\tau(1)},\cdots,x_{\tau(j_{1})}),\cdots,f_{j_{l}}(x_{\tau(k-(j_{1}+\cdots+j_{l-1}))},\cdots,x_{\tau(k)})\Bigr)

on homogeneous elements
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in\mathbf{V}
coincide.

The morphism
F=\bigl(f_{k}\colon S^{k}\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{W}\bigr)_{k\geq 1}
of
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebras is an
isomorphism
of
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebras if
f_{1}
is an isomorphism of vector spaces.

Example 2.5
.

A dgLa structure
([\cdot,\cdot],\mathbf{d})
on a graded vector space
\mathbf{V}
becomes a dgL
[1]
a structure
(m_{1},m_{2})
on
\mathbf{V}[1]
by setting
m_{1}=-\mathbf{d}
,
m_{k}=0
for
k>2
, and by defining
m_{2}
by

m_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})=(-1)^{|x_{1}|}[x_{1},x_{2}]

for degree-homogeneous elements
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbf{V}
, where
|x_{1}|
is the degree of
x_{1}
as an element of
\mathbf{V}
.

Example 2.6
.

(Lie 2-algebra
[
1
]
)
A
2-term
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra
is a
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra defined on
a graded
1
1

1

The peculiar choice of grading versus indices of the summands becomes clear at the end of this example.
vector bundle
\mathbf{V}=\mathfrak{g}_{0}[1]\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-1}[2]
.
This amounts to a 2-term cochain complex of vector spaces
\mathfrak{g}_{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathfrak{g}_{0}
together with

•

a skew-symmetric bilinear map
m_{2}^{0}\colon\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\to\mathfrak{g}_{0}
(this map is also written
m_{2}^{0}=[\cdot,\cdot]
)

•

a bilinear map
m_{2}^{1}\colon\mathfrak{g}_{0}\otimes\mathfrak{g}_{-1}\to\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
(this map is also written
m_{2}^{1}=\nabla
)

•

an alternating trilinear map
m_{3}\colon\wedge^{3}\mathfrak{g}_{0}\to\mathfrak{g}_{-1}

satisfying, for all
x,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
and
y,y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
, the following conditions:

(i)

m_{1}(\nabla_{x}y)=[x,m_{1}(y)]

(ii)

\nabla_{m_{1}(y_{1})}y_{2}+\nabla_{m_{1}(y_{2})}y_{1}=0

(iii)

[x_{1},[x_{2},x_{3}]]+[x_{2},[x_{3},x_{1}]]+[x_{3},[x_{1},x_{2}]]=m_{1}m_{3}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})

(iv)

\nabla_{x_{1}}\nabla_{x_{2}}y-\nabla_{x_{2}}\nabla_{x_{1}}y-\nabla_{[x_{1},x_{2}]}y=m_{3}(x_{1},x_{2},m_{1}(y))

(v)

and

\begin{split}0=&\sum_{i=1}^{4}(-1)^{i+1}\nabla_{x_{i}}(m_{3}(x_{1},\ldots,\widehat{x_{i}},\ldots,x_{4}))+\sum_{i<j}(-1)^{i+j}m_{3}([x_{i},x_{j}],x_{1},\ldots,\widehat{x_{i}},\ldots,\widehat{x_{j}},\ldots,x_{4}).\end{split}

In other words,
\mathfrak{g}_{0}[0]\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-1}[1]
with
(m_{1},m_{2}=[\cdot,\cdot]+\nabla,m_{3})
is a
Lie 2-algebra
. The maps
m_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, are usually written
l_{i}
in this context.

Remark 2.7
.

(1)

In general, a
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra becomes a
L_{\infty}
-algebra

[
26
,
27
]
when shifted appropriately by
-1
, see
[
46
,
8
]
. Because the setting of
L_{\infty}
-algebras is not used in this paper, only
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebras are defined and considered.

(2)

A Lie 2-algebra with
l_{3}=0
is called a
strict Lie 2-algebra
. Then by the equations above,
[\cdot,\cdot]
is a Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
and
\nabla
is a representation of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
on
\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
. (ii) above defines a skew-symmetric map
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{g}_{-1}}\colon\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}_{-1}\to\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
,
[y_{1},y_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}_{-1}}=\nabla_{l_{1}(y_{1})}y_{2}
which, by (iii), satisfies the Jacobi identity. (i) shows that
l_{1}\colon\mathfrak{g}_{-1}\to\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is a morphism of Lie algebras.
(i) and (iii) then imply as well together that
\nabla\colon\mathfrak{g}_{0}\to\mathfrak{aut}(\mathfrak{g}_{-1})
, i.e.
\nabla
is a representation of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
by derivations of
\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
. Hence
\left(\mathfrak{g}_{0},\mathfrak{g}_{-1},l_{1},\nabla\right)
is a
crossed-module of Lie algebras
in the following sense.

(3)

A
crossed module of Lie algebras
is a pair of Lie algebras
(\mathfrak{g}_{-1},\mathfrak{g}_{0})
together with a Lie algebra morphism
\phi\colon\mathfrak{g}_{-1}\to\mathfrak{g}_{0}
and a Lie algebra action by derivations
\psi\colon\mathfrak{g}_{0}\to\mathfrak{aut}(\mathfrak{g}_{-1})
satisfying, for any
x\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
, the following two conditions:

\phi(\psi_{x}(y_{1}))=[x,\phi(y_{1})]_{\mathfrak{g}_{0}}\quad\text{and}\quad\psi_{\phi(y_{1})}(y_{2})=[y_{1},y_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}_{-1}}.

The above considerations establish an equivalence between crossed-modules of Lie algebras and strict Lie 2-algebras.

Example 2.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra and let
\mathfrak{i}
be an ideal in
\mathfrak{g}
. Then
\mathfrak{g}[0]\oplus\mathfrak{i}[-1]
becomes a strict Lie 2-algebra with the inclusion
l_{1}\colon\mathfrak{i}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}
, the Lie bracket
l_{2}^{0}=[\cdot\,,\cdot]
on
\mathfrak{g}
and the restriction to
\mathfrak{i}
of the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{g}
:
l_{2}^{1}=\ad\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{i}\to\mathfrak{i}
,
l_{2}^{1}(x\otimes y)=[x,y]\in\mathfrak{i}
for
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and
y\in\mathfrak{i}
.

Voronov introduced in
[
46
]
a construction of
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebras, see also
[
10
,
9
]
for the following approach to it.

Definition 2.9
.

A quadruple
(L,\mathfrak{a},P,\Theta)
where

(1)

L=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}L_{i}[-i]
is a graded Lie algebra with Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
,

(2)

\mathfrak{a}
is an abelian graded Lie subalgebra of
L
,

(3)

P\colon L\to\mathfrak{a}
is a linear projection such that
\Ker P
is a graded Lie subalgebra,

(4)

\Theta
is an element of
L_{1}[-1]
(i.e. of degree
1
) such that
\Theta\in\Ker P
and
[\Theta,\Theta]=0

is called here a
Voronov-dataset
.

When
\Theta\in L_{1}[-1]
is not assumed to live inside
\Ker P
, but only to commute with itself, i.e.
[\Theta,\Theta]=0
, the quadruple
(L,\mathfrak{a},P,\Theta)
is called a
curved Voronov dataset
.

Voronov proves in
[
46
,
45
]
the following two theorems.

Theorem 2.10

(
[
46
]
)
.

Let
(L,\mathfrak{a},P,\Theta)
be a Voronov-dataset. The multibrackets
m_{k}\colon S^{k}\mathfrak{a}\to\mathfrak{a}[1]
given by

m_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})=P[[\ldots[[\Theta,a_{1}],a_{2}],\ldots],a_{k}],

for all
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\mathfrak{a}
determine an
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra structure on
\mathfrak{a}
.

Voronov’s version of the following theorem in
[
45
]
is more general since it considers general derivations of the graded Lie algebra. The following version is stated in
[
10
]
and focuses on inner derivations.

Theorem 2.11

(
[
45
,
10
]
)
.

Given a Voronov-dataset
(L,\mathfrak{a},P,\Theta)
, set
D:=[\Theta,\cdot]
. The following brackets induce an
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra structure on the space
L[1]\oplus\mathfrak{a}
:

(1)

The unary bracket is given by
m_{1}(x,a)=(-(Dx),P(x+Da))
for
x\in L
and
a\in\mathfrak{a}
.

(2)

The binary bracket is given by
2
2

2

Here,
|x|
is the degree of
x
as an element of
L
, and so its degree as an element of
L[1]
is
|x|-1
.

m_{2}(x,y)=(-1)^{|x|}[x,y]
for
x,y\in L
.

(3)

For
k\geq 2
the
k
-ary bracket is given by

m_{k}(x,a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1})=P[\dots[x,a_{1}],\dots,a_{k-1}]

and

m_{k}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})=P[\dots[Da_{1},a_{2}],\dots,a_{k}]

for
x\in L
and
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in\mathfrak{a}
, and it vanishes on any different combination of elements of
L[1]\oplus\mathfrak{a}
.

2.2.
Deformation complexes

This section only considers finite-dimensional real Lie algebras.
Given a Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{g},\mu\coloneqq[\cdot,\cdot])
and a representation
r\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(V)
of
\mathfrak{g}
on some vector space
V
, there is an associated complex
C^{\bullet}_{r}(\mathfrak{g};V)\coloneqq\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes V
called the
Chevalley-Eilenberg complex with coefficients in
V
. Its differential
\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{r}\colon C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};V)\to C^{\bullet+1}(\mathfrak{g};V)
is defined for all
k\geq 0
and
\omega\in C^{k}(\mathfrak{g};V)
by

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{r}\omega(x_{1},\dots,x_{k+1})\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}(-1)^{i+1}r(x_{i})\left(\omega\left(x_{1},\dots,\widehat{x_{i}},\dots,x_{k+1}\right)\right)

(4)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i<j}(-1)^{i+j}\omega\left([x_{i},x_{j}],x_{1},\dots,\widehat{x_{i}},\dots,\widehat{x_{j}},\dots,x_{k+1}\right).

The associated cohomology is denoted by
H_{r}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};V)
.

Remark 2.12
.

The assignment

\{\mathfrak{g}\text{-modules}\}\to\{\text{cochain complexes}\},\qquad(V,r)\mapsto\big(C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};V),\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{r}\big),

extends to a functor from the category of
\mathfrak{g}
-modules to the category of cochain complexes: it sends a
\mathfrak{g}
-module morphism
\phi\colon(V,r_{V})\to(W,r_{W})
3
3

3

I.e.
\phi\colon V\to W
is a linear map such that
\phi\circ r_{V}=r_{W}\circ\phi
.
to
\phi_{*}\colon\big(C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};V),\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{r_{V}}\big)\to\big(C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};W),\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{r_{W}}\big)
defined by
\phi_{*}(\omega)=\phi\circ\omega
for
\omega\in C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};V)
. It is easy to check that this functor is exact, i.e. that it sends short exact sequences to short exact sequences.

Section
3
illustrates how strongly the complexes in the following list are linked with deformation theories in the context of Lie algebras (see
[
4
]
and references therein).

(1)

The
deformation complex of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex
C^{\bullet}_{\rm ad}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g}):=\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
with the representation
\ad\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})
. The corresponding differential is accordingly denoted by
\delta^{\ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}
and its cohomology is written
H^{\bullet}_{\rm ad}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})
.

(2)

The
deformation complex of a Lie algebra morphism
\phi\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}
is given by the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex
C^{\bullet}_{\rm def}(\phi):=\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{h}
with the representation
\phi^{*}\ad^{\mathfrak{h}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{h})
. The corresponding differential is here denoted by
\delta_{\phi}
for simplicity and its cohomology by
H^{\bullet}_{\phi}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{h})
.

(3)

The
deformation complex of a Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is given by the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex
C^{\bullet}_{\rm def}(\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}):=\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
with the
Bott representation

{\rm ad}^{\text{Bott}}\colon\mathfrak{h}\to\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}),\quad{\rm ad}^{\text{Bott}}_{u}(\bar{y})=\overline{[u,y]},

where
\overline{(\cdot)}
denotes the image under the canonical projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h},\ x\mapsto\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}(x)\eqqcolon\overline{x}
. The corresponding differential is denoted by
\delta^{\rm Bott}_{\mathfrak{h}}
and its cohomology is written
H^{\bullet}_{\rm Bott}(\mathfrak{h};\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})=:H^{\bullet}_{\rm def}(\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g})
.

The following result is an inspiration for similar results on the relations between the different cohomologies associated to ideal in Lie algebras, see Section
4.2
.

Remark 2.13
.

(1)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra and let
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie subalgebra.
The monomorphism
\iota\colon\mathfrak{h}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}
of Lie algebras induces the following short exact sequence of cochain complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\bullet}_{\rm ad}(\mathfrak{h};\mathfrak{h})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{*}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}C^{\bullet}_{\rm def}(\iota)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}})_{*}}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}C^{\bullet}_{\rm def}(\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g})}

where
\iota_{*}(\phi)(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=\iota(\phi(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}))
and
(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}})_{*}(\psi)(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}(\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}))
for
\phi\in\wedge^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{h}
,
\psi\in\wedge^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathfrak{h}
.
The equalities

\iota_{*}\circ\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}=\delta_{\iota}\circ\iota_{*}\quad\text{ and }\quad(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}})_{*}\circ\delta_{\iota}=\delta^{\text{Bott}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}})_{*}

are immediate. This observation is a direct consequence of Remark
2.12
.

(2)

Consider the cokernel complex

\Coker^{\bullet}(\iota_{*})\coloneqq\frac{C^{\bullet}_{\rm def}(\iota)}{C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{h};\mathfrak{h})}=\frac{\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}}{\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{h}}

with the differential
\overline{\delta_{\iota}}
defined by the equation
\overline{\delta_{\iota}}\circ p=p\circ\delta_{\iota}
, where

p\colon C_{\rm def}^{\bullet}(\iota)\to\Coker^{\bullet}(\iota_{*})

is the quotient map. The cokernel complex is then canonically isomorphic to the deformation complex of Lie subalgebras
C^{\bullet}_{\rm def}(\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g})
, since the category of cochain complexes is an abelian category
[
47
, Theorem 1.2.3]
and so, in particular, each epimorphism, as
(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}})_{*}
, is canonically isomorphic to the cokernel of its kernel
[
47
, Definition 1.2.1 and 1.2.2]
.

2.3.
Lie algebraic structures as Maurer-Cartan elements

This section collects
two situations, the ones of Lie algebras and of Lie algebras with representations,
where the considered Lie structures are realised as Maurer-Cartan elements in appropriate graded Lie algebras, and their deformations then become Maurer-Cartan elements in the obtained differential graded Lie algebras.

2.3.1.
Lie algebras as Maurer-Cartan elements
[
34
]

Consider a vector space
V
and the graded Lie algebra
L\coloneqq C^{\bullet}(V;V)[1]=\wedge^{\bullet+1}V^{*}\otimes V
(recall that this notation means that the elements of
\wedge^{r+1}V^{*}\otimes V
have degree
r
) with the graded Lie algebra bracket given by

(5)

\displaystyle\llbracket\xi,\eta\rrbracket=(-1)^{(r-1)\cdot(s-1)}\xi\circ\eta-\eta\circ\xi\in\wedge^{r+s-1}V^{*}\otimes V=(C^{\bullet}(V;V)[1])_{r+s-2}

on tensors
\xi\in\wedge^{r}V^{*}\otimes V=(C^{\bullet}(V;V)[1])_{r-1}
and
\eta\in\wedge^{s}V^{*}\otimes V=(C^{\bullet}(V;V)[1])_{s-1}

(of degrees
r-1
, and
s-1
, respectively),
where:

\displaystyle(\xi\circ\eta)(x_{1},\dots,x_{r+s-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in S_{(s,r-1)}}(-1)^{\tau}\xi(\eta(x_{\tau(1)},\dots,x_{\tau(s))}),x_{\tau(s+1)},\dots,x_{\tau(r+s-1)})

on all
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r+s-1}\in V
.
A simple computation using (
5
) shows that a degree
1
-element
\mu\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}\otimes V
satisfies
\llbracket\mu,\mu\rrbracket=-2\cdot\operatorname{Jac}_{\mu}
, hence

(6)

\llbracket\mu,\mu\rrbracket=0\quad\text{ if and only if }\quad\mu\text{ is a Lie bracket on }V.

The operator
\llbracket\mu,\cdot\rrbracket\colon\wedge^{\bullet+1}V^{*}\otimes V\to\wedge^{\bullet+2}V^{*}\otimes V
is then the Chevalley-Eilenberg differential
\delta^{\ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}
defined by the Lie bracket
\mu
, and
(C^{\bullet}(V;V)[1],\llbracket\cdot\,,\cdot\rrbracket,\delta^{\ad}_{\mathfrak{g}})
is a dgLa. This follows also in a straightforward manner from the formula (
5
).

It is then immediate that for
\widetilde{\mu}\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}\otimes V
, the sum
\mu+\widetilde{\mu}
is a Lie algebra bracket on
V
if and only if

\llbracket\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{\mu}\rrbracket+2\delta^{\ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\widetilde{\mu})=0,

i.e. if and only if
\widetilde{\mu}
is a Maurer-Cartan element of the differential graded Lie algebra
(C^{\bullet}(V;V)[1],\llbracket\cdot\,,\cdot\rrbracket,\delta^{\ad}_{\mathfrak{g}})
.

2.3.2.
Lie algebras and representations as Maurer-Cartan elements
[
10
]

Now, let
V
and
W
be two real and finite-dimensional vector spaces and consider

L\coloneqq C^{\bullet}(V,V)[1]\oplus C^{\bullet}(V,\operatorname{End}(W))

That is, the space of elements of degree
p\geq 0
of
L
is

\left(\wedge^{p+1}V^{*}\otimes V\right)\oplus\left(\wedge^{p}V^{*}\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)\right)

and

V\simeq\wedge^{0}V^{*}\otimes V

is the space of elements of degree
-1
of
L
.
Then
L
is equipped with a graded Lie bracket defined as follows. Choose
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\wedge^{\bullet+1}V^{*}\otimes V
of degrees
p
and
p^{\prime}
, and
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\wedge^{\bullet}V^{*}\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)
of degrees
q
and
q^{\prime}
, respectively. Then

(7)

\displaystyle\llbracket\xi,\xi^{\prime}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p\cdot p^{\prime}}\xi\circ\xi^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime}\circ\xi\in\wedge^{p+p^{\prime}+1}V^{*}\otimes V

\displaystyle\llbracket\xi,\eta\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\eta\circ\xi\in\wedge^{p+q}V^{*}\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)&\text{ if }q\geq 1\\
\quad 0&\text{ if }q=0\end{array}\right.

(10)

\displaystyle\llbracket\eta,\eta^{\prime}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=(-1)^{qq^{\prime}}\eta\circ\eta^{\prime}-\eta^{\prime}\circ\eta\in\wedge^{q+q^{\prime}}V^{*}\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)

where
\xi\circ\xi^{\prime}
is defined as in (
5
) and

(11)

\displaystyle(\eta\circ\xi)(x_{1},\dots,x_{p+q})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in S_{(p+1,q-1)}}(-1)^{\tau}\eta(\xi(x_{\tau(1)},\dots,x_{\tau(p+1)}),x_{\tau(p+2)},\dots,x_{\tau(p+q)})

(12)

\displaystyle(\eta\circ\eta^{\prime})(x_{1},\dots,x_{q+q^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in S_{(q^{\prime},q)}}(-1)^{\tau}\eta(x_{\tau(q^{\prime}+1)},\dots,x_{\tau(q+q^{\prime})})\circ\eta^{\prime}(x_{\tau(1)},\dots,x_{\tau(q^{\prime})})

on
x_{i}\in V
.
Consider an element
\mu+\rho\in\left(\wedge^{2}V^{*}\otimes V\right)\oplus\left(\wedge^{1}V^{*}\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)\right)
of degree
1
in
L
.
Then

\left\llbracket\mu+\rho,\mu+\rho\right\rrbracket=(-2\mu\circ\mu)+(-2\rho\circ\mu-2\rho\circ\rho)=(-2\operatorname{Jac}_{\mu})+(-2\rho\circ\mu-2\rho\circ\rho).

Compute on
x_{1},x_{2}\in V

\begin{split}(-\rho\circ\mu-\rho\circ\rho)(x_{1},x_{2})=-\rho(\mu(x_{1},x_{2}))-\rho(x_{2})\circ\rho(x_{1})+\rho(x_{1})\circ\rho(x_{2}).\end{split}

This shows that
\left\llbracket\mu+\rho,\mu+\rho\right\rrbracket=0
if and only if
\mu
is a Lie bracket on
V
and
\rho
is a representation of
(V,\mu)
on
W
.

As before, the operator
\delta=\llbracket\mu+\rho,\cdot\rrbracket
on
L
makes
(L,\llbracket\cdot\,,\cdot\rrbracket,\delta)
into a differential graded Lie algebra.
It is then immediate that for
\widetilde{\mu}\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}\otimes V
and
\widetilde{\rho}\in\wedge^{1}V^{*}\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)
, the sum
\mu+\widetilde{\mu}
is a Lie algebra bracket on
V
such that
\rho+\widetilde{\rho}
is a representation of
(V,\mu+\widetilde{\mu})
on
W
if and only if

0=\llbracket(\mu+\widetilde{\mu})+(\rho+\widetilde{\rho}),(\mu+\widetilde{\mu})+(\rho+\widetilde{\rho})\rrbracket=\llbracket\widetilde{\mu}+\widetilde{\rho},\widetilde{\mu}+\widetilde{\rho}\rrbracket+2\delta_{\mu+\rho}(\widetilde{\mu}+\widetilde{\rho}),

i.e. if and only if
\widetilde{\mu}+\widetilde{\rho}
is a Maurer-Cartan element of the differential graded Lie algebra
(L,\llbracket\cdot\,,\cdot\rrbracket,\delta_{\mu+\rho})
.

The following remark about the composition defined in (
12
) is useful later on in this paper.

Remark 2.14
.

(1)

For
\eta=\omega\otimes\phi
and
\eta^{\prime}=\omega^{\prime}\otimes\phi^{\prime}
with
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\wedge^{\bullet}V^{*}
and
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\End(W)
,

\eta\circ\eta^{\prime}=(\omega\otimes\phi)\circ(\omega^{\prime}\otimes\phi^{\prime})=(\omega\wedge\omega^{\prime})\otimes(\phi\circ\phi^{\prime}).

Hence the operation
\circ
defined in (
12
) is associative and for
\eta,\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}\in\wedge^{\bullet}V^{*}\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)
of degrees
q,q^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime\prime}
, respectively, the composition
(\eta\circ\eta^{\prime}\circ\eta^{\prime\prime})(x_{1},\dots,x_{q+q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}})
equals

(13)

\sum_{\tau\in S_{(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime},q)}}(-1)^{\tau}\eta(x_{\tau(1+q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime})},\dots,x_{\tau(q+q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime})})\circ\eta^{\prime}(x_{\tau(1+q^{\prime\prime})},\dots,x_{\tau(q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime})})\circ\eta^{\prime\prime}(x_{\tau(1)},\dots,x_{\tau(q^{\prime\prime})}),

for all
x_{1},\ldots,x_{q+q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}}\in V
, where
S_{(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime},q)}\subset S_{q+q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}}
is the subgroup of
(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime},q)
-shuffles, i.e. permutations
\tau\in S_{q+q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}}
such that

\tau(1)<\ldots<\tau(q^{\prime\prime}),\quad\tau(1+q^{\prime\prime})<\ldots<\tau(q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime})\quad\text{ and }\quad\tau(1+q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime})<\ldots<\tau(q+q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}).

(2)

For vector spaces
W_{1},W_{2}
and
W_{3}
, the composition
\eta\circ\eta^{\prime}\in\wedge^{q+q^{\prime}}V^{*}\otimes\Hom(W_{1},W_{3})
of a form
\eta\in\wedge^{q}\Hom(W_{2},W_{3})
with
\eta^{\prime}\in\wedge^{q^{\prime}}V^{*}\otimes\Hom(W_{1},W_{2})
is defined as in (
12
).

(3)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra and let
r_{U}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(U)
,
r_{V}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(V)
and
r_{W}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(W)
be three representations of
\mathfrak{g}
.
Equip the vector spaces
\Hom(U,V)
,
\Hom(V,W)
and
\Hom(U,W)
with the induced representations, all written
r^{\Hom}
for simplicity.
Then the linear composition map

\circ\colon\Hom(V,W)\otimes\Hom(U,V)\to\Hom(U,W),\qquad\phi\otimes\psi\mapsto\phi\circ\psi

is a morphism of representations, i.e.

r^{\Hom}_{x}(\phi\circ\psi)=\left(r^{\Hom}_{x}\phi\right)\circ\psi+\phi\circ\left(r^{\Hom}_{x}\psi\right)

for all
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and all
\phi\in\Hom(V,W)
,
\psi\in\Hom(U,V)
.

As a consequence, the induced Chevalley-Eilenberg operators

\delta^{\Hom}\colon C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},\Hom(V,W))\to C^{\bullet+1}(\mathfrak{g},\Hom(V,W)),

\delta^{\Hom}\colon C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},\Hom(U,V))\to C^{\bullet+1}(\mathfrak{g},\Hom(U,V)),

and

\delta^{\Hom}\colon C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},\Hom(U,W))\to C^{\bullet+1}(\mathfrak{g},\Hom(U,W)),

satisfy

\delta^{\Hom}(\eta\circ\eta^{\prime})=\left(\delta^{\Hom}\eta\right)\circ\eta^{\prime}+(-1)^{|\eta|}\eta\circ\left(\delta^{\Hom}\eta^{\prime}\right)

for all
\eta\in C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},\Hom(V,W))
and
\eta^{\prime}\in C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},\Hom(U,V))
. The proof is an easy computation that is left to the reader.

3.
Deformation theory of Lie subalgebras – recap

This section recalls, to the extent needed for the purposes of this paper, the deformation theory of Lie subalgebras
[
4
,
10
]
.
Let
\mathfrak{h}
be a
k
-dimensional Lie subalgebra of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and let
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
be the Grassmannian of
k
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
smooth deformation
(\mathfrak{h}_{t})_{t\in I}
of a Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
inside a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is a smooth curve
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}:[0,1]\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
such that
\mathfrak{h}_{t}\coloneqq\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}(t)
is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
for all
t\in I\coloneqq[0,1]
and such that
\mathfrak{h}_{0}=\mathfrak{h}
.

Two smooth deformations
(\mathfrak{h}_{t})_{t\in I}
and
(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{t})_{t\in I}
of
\mathfrak{h}
inside
\mathfrak{g}
are called
equivalent
if there exists a smooth curve
g\colon I\to G
(the unique simply-connected integration of
\mathfrak{g}
), starting at the identity and such that
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{t}=\Ad_{g(t)}\mathfrak{h}_{t}
for each
t\in I
.

Appendix
A.1

explains in detail how the tangent vectors to
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
at
\mathfrak{h}
are computed and seen as elements of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}\simeq T_{\mathfrak{h}}\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
.
In short, a choice of linear complement
\mathfrak{h}^{c}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
for
\mathfrak{h}
in
\mathfrak{g}
defines a smooth chart of
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
centered at
\mathfrak{h}
. Precisely, the map
\Psi\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{c})\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
sending
\phi
to
\grap(\phi)
is a diffeomorphism on its image
U_{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{c}}:=\{W\subseteq\mathfrak{g}\mid W\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{c}=\mathfrak{g}\}\subseteq\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
. The inverse
\Psi^{-1}\colon U_{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{c}}\to\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{h},{\mathfrak{h}^{c}})
sends
\mathfrak{h}
to
0
.

Via this smooth chart,
I\ni t\mapsto\mathfrak{h}_{t}\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})

coincides with a smooth curve
\phi\colon I\to\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{c})
, i.e.
\mathfrak{h}_{t}=\grap(\phi(t))
for all
t\in I
.
(Since only the values of
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}
in an arbitrarily small neighborhood of
0
in
I
are relevant, assume without loss of generality that
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}
has values in
U_{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{c}}
.) Since
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{c})
is a vector space, the derivative
\dot{\phi}(0)
is again an element of
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{c})=\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{h^{c}}
. A composition with
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
defines then

\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\circ\dot{\phi}(0)=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}\mathfrak{h}_{t}

as an element of
T_{\mathfrak{h}}\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\simeq\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}=C^{1}_{\rm def}(\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g})
.

Proposition 3.2

(
[
4
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be a
k
-dimensional Lie subalgebra of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. If
(\mathfrak{h}_{t})_{t\in I}
is a smooth deformation of
\mathfrak{h}
inside
\mathfrak{g}
, then

\dot{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\coloneqq\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\mathfrak{h}_{t}\in C^{1}_{\rm def}(\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g})

is a cocycle. Moreover, the corresponding cohomology class only depends on the equivalence class of the deformation.

Proof.

Consider as above
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}
to be a smooth curve in
\phi\colon I\to\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{h},{\mathfrak{h}^{c}})
for a linear complement
{\mathfrak{h}^{c}}
of
\mathfrak{h}
in
\mathfrak{g}
and set

\alpha\colon I\to\GL(\mathfrak{g}),\qquad t\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}\id_{\mathfrak{h}}&0\\
\phi(t)&\id_{\mathfrak{h}^{c}}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathfrak{g}\simeq\mathfrak{h}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}^{c}}
. Then
\alpha(t)(\mathfrak{h})=\grap(\phi(t))=\mathfrak{h}_{t}
and

\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\circ\dot{\alpha}(0)\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{h}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\circ\dot{\phi}(0)=\dot{\mathfrak{h}}_{0},

see Appendix
A.1
.
Set similarly
\pi_{t}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h_{t}}
to be the canonical projection for each
t\in I
. Since
(\pi_{t}\circ\alpha(t))(\mathfrak{h})=\pi_{t}(\mathfrak{h}_{t})=0
for all
t\in I
,
\alpha(t)
factors for each
t
to
\overline{\alpha(t)}:=\overline{\alpha}(t)
as in the following commutative diagram.

(14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha}(t)}

The maps
\overline{\alpha}(t)
are isomorphisms for all
t\in I
. In order to see this, note that
for
x\in\mathfrak{g}
,
\overline{\alpha}(t)(x+\mathfrak{h})=0
if and only if
\alpha(t)(x)\in\mathfrak{h}_{t}
. Since
\alpha(t)
is bijective with
\alpha(t)(\mathfrak{h})=\mathfrak{h}_{t}
, this is the case if and only if
x\in\mathfrak{h}
.

Since
\mathfrak{h}_{t}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is a Lie subalgebra for each
t\in I
the map
\sigma\colon I\to\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
defined for all
t\in I
by

\sigma(t)(x,y)=\overline{\alpha}(t)^{-1}\circ\pi_{t}[\alpha(t)(x),\alpha(t)(y)]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{commutative diagram in the proof of the cocycle in Lie subalgebras!}}}{{=}}\pi\circ\alpha(t)^{-1}[\alpha(t)(x),\alpha(t)(y)]

for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{h}
,
vanishes identically on
I
. Hence, differentiating
t\mapsto\sigma(t)(x,y)
at
t=0
for
x,y\in\mathfrak{h}
yields

(15)

-\pi\circ\dot{\alpha}(0)[x,y]+\pi[\dot{\alpha}(0)(x),y]+\pi[x,\dot{\alpha}(0)(y)]=0,

which is exactly the cocycle condition
\delta^{\text{Bott}}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\dot{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})=\delta^{\text{Bott}}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\circ\dot{\alpha}(0)\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{h}})=0
.

Let
(\mathfrak{h}_{t})_{t\in I}
and
(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{t})_{t\in I}
be two equivalent deformations of a Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
of
\mathfrak{g}
and let

g\colon I\to G
be the smooth curve with
g(0)=e
and
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{t}=\Ad_{g(t)}\mathfrak{h}_{t}
for all
t\in I
.
Then
\Ad_{g(t)}\circ\alpha(t)=:\alpha^{\prime}(t)
defines a smooth curve
\alpha^{\prime}\colon I\to\GL(\mathfrak{g})
starting at the identity with
\alpha^{\prime}(t)(\mathfrak{h})=\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{t}
for all
t\in I
. A differentiation at
t=0
yields

[\dot{g}(0),x]+\dot{\alpha}(0)(x)=\dot{\alpha^{\prime}}(0)(x)

for all
x\in\mathfrak{g}
.
Applying the projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
then leads to
\dot{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}-\dot{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}_{0}=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\circ\dot{\alpha}(0)\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{h}}-\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\circ\dot{\alpha^{\prime}}(0)\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{h}}=\delta^{\text{Bott}}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\dot{g}(0))
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Similarly, a deformation of a Lie algebra morphism gives as follows a
deformation cocycle
, see
[
4
]
, and references therein. Let
\phi_{0}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}
be a morphism of Lie algebras and let
\phi\colon I\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{h}
be a smooth curve defined on an open interval
I
containing
0
, such that
\phi(t)\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}
is a morphism of Lie algebras for all
t\in I
, and such that
\phi(0)=\phi_{0}
.

Then
\dot{\phi}(0)=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}\phi(t)
is again an element in the vector space
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{h}
and for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}

\begin{split}\delta_{\phi_{0}}\left(\dot{\phi}(0)\right)(x,y)&=\left[\phi_{0}(x),\dot{\phi}(0)(y)\right]-\left[\phi_{0}(y),\dot{\phi}(0)(x)\right]-\dot{\phi}(0)([x,y])\\
&=\left[\phi(0)(x),\dot{\phi}(0)(y)\right]+\left[\dot{\phi}(0)(x),\phi(0)(y)\right]-\dot{\phi}(0)([x,y])\\
&=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}\left(\left[\phi(t)(x),\phi(t)(y)\right]-\phi(t)([x,y])\right)=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}0=0.\end{split}

That is,
\dot{\phi}(0)\in Z^{1}_{\phi_{0}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
. The element
\dot{\phi}(0)
is called the
deformation cocycle
of the Lie algebra morphism
\phi\colon\mathfrak{h}\to\mathfrak{g}
associated to
(\phi_{t})_{t\in I}
.

The following Voronov-dataset due to
[
10
]
is used for constructing the controlling L
{}_{\infty}[1]
-algebra of small deformations of a Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
.
Here, instead of considering a linear complement

{\mathfrak{h}^{c}}
of
\mathfrak{h}
in
\mathfrak{g}
,
consider the equivalent splitting

\sigma\colon\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}

of the short exact sequence

(16)

0\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}\rightarrow 0

of vector spaces defined by the epimorphism
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
of vector spaces.
Denote by
p_{\sigma}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}
,
x\mapsto x-\sigma(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}(x))
the projection associated to this choice of splitting.
The inclusion of
\mathfrak{h}
in
\mathfrak{g}
is written
i_{\mathfrak{h}}\colon\mathfrak{h}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}
in order to distinguish it from the contraction operator
\iota
.

Lemma 3.4
.

In the situation above, the following quadruple defines a Voronov-dataset:

•

L\coloneqq C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})[1]=(\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})[1]
with the graded Lie algebra bracket given as in (
5
):

\llbracket\phi\otimes v,\psi\otimes w\rrbracket=(-1)^{(r-1)\cdot(s-1)}\psi\wedge\iota_{w}\phi\otimes v-\phi\wedge\iota_{v}\psi\otimes w

on elementary tensors
\phi\otimes v\in\wedge^{r}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
and
\psi\otimes w\in\wedge^{s}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}

(of degrees
r-1
, and
s-1
, respectively).

•

\mathfrak{a}\coloneqq C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{h};{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}})[1]=\wedge^{\bullet+1}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}
, with the inclusion
I\colon\mathfrak{a}\to L
given by
I(\psi\otimes v)=p_{\sigma}^{*}\psi\otimes\sigma(v)
.

•

P\colon L\to\mathfrak{a}
the projection given by:
\phi\otimes x\mapsto i_{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}\phi\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}(x)
with kernel given by

\Ker P=\big(\wedge^{\bullet+1}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{h}\big)+\big(\mathfrak{h}^{\circ}\wedge(\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{*})\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\big).

•

\Theta=\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
is the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{g}
, which, as an element of
\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
, has degree
1
, and satisfies
\llbracket\mu_{\mathfrak{g}},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\rrbracket=0
by (
6
).

Proof.

The subspace
\mathfrak{a}
is an abelian Lie subalgebra of
L
, because for

\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\wedge^{\bullet+1}\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and
v_{1},v_{2}\in{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}

(17)

\displaystyle I(\psi_{1}\otimes v_{1})\circ I(\psi_{2}\otimes v_{2})=p_{\sigma}^{*}(\psi_{2})\wedge\iota_{\sigma(v_{2})}(p_{\sigma}^{*}\psi_{1})\otimes\sigma(v_{1})=0,

using
p_{\sigma}\circ\sigma=0
.
Next verify that
\Ker P
is a Lie subalgebra of
L
. The following computations show that the bracket of any two elements in
\Ker P
lies again in
\Ker P
.
For
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\wedge^{\bullet+1}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}
,

\llbracket\phi_{1}\otimes u_{1},\phi_{2}\otimes u_{2}\rrbracket=(-1)^{|\phi_{1}||\phi_{2}|}\phi_{2}\wedge\iota_{u_{2}}\phi_{1}\otimes u_{1}-\phi_{1}\wedge\iota_{u_{1}}{\phi_{2}}\otimes u_{2}\\

is again an element of
\wedge^{\bullet+1}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{h}
. For
\psi_{1}\in\wedge^{r+1}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
,
u\in\mathfrak{h}
,
\alpha\in\mathfrak{h}^{\circ}
,
\psi_{2}\in\wedge^{s}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
v\in{\mathfrak{g}}

\begin{split}&\llbracket\psi_{1}\otimes u,\alpha\wedge\psi_{2}\otimes v\rrbracket=(-1)^{rs}\alpha\wedge\psi_{2}\wedge\iota_{v}\psi_{1}\otimes u-\underset{=0}{\underbrace{\psi_{1}\wedge(\iota_{u}\alpha)\wedge\psi_{2}\otimes v}}+\psi_{1}\wedge\alpha\wedge\iota_{u}\psi_{2}\otimes v\end{split}

is an element of
\mathfrak{h}^{\circ}\wedge(\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{*})\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq\ker P
.
For
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
,
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}^{\circ}
and
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}
,

\begin{split}\llbracket\alpha_{1}\wedge\psi_{1}\otimes v_{1},\alpha_{2}\wedge\psi_{2}\otimes v_{2}\rrbracket&=(-1)^{|\psi_{1}|\cdot|\psi_{2}|}\alpha_{2}\wedge\psi_{2}\wedge\iota_{v_{2}}(\alpha_{1}\wedge\psi_{1})\otimes v_{1}-\alpha_{1}\wedge\psi_{1}\wedge\iota_{v_{1}}(\alpha_{2}\wedge\psi_{2})\otimes v_{2}\end{split},

which is again an element of
\mathfrak{h}^{\circ}\wedge(\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{*})\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Finally note that
P(\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})(u_{1},u_{2})=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}[u_{1},u_{2}]
for all
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}
. Therefore,
\Theta=\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\in\Ker P
if and only if
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is a Lie subalgebra. As seen before,
\llbracket\mu_{\mathfrak{g}},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\rrbracket=0
since
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
is the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{g}
.
∎

The multibrackets of the
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra on
\mathfrak{a}
induced by the above Voronov-dataset, are given by

m_{l}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l})=P\llbracket\llbracket\ldots\llbracket\llbracket\mu_{\mathfrak{g}},I(a_{1})\rrbracket,I(a_{2})\rrbracket,\ldots\rrbracket,I(a_{l})\rrbracket

for
l\geq 1
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\in\mathfrak{a}
.

First recall that
\llbracket\mu_{\mathfrak{g}},I(a)\rrbracket=\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}(I(a))
for
a\in\mathfrak{a}
. Hence, for
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\in\mathfrak{a}

\begin{split}&\llbracket\llbracket\llbracket\llbracket\mu_{\mathfrak{g}},I(a_{1})\rrbracket,I(a_{2})\rrbracket,I(a_{3})\rrbracket,I(a_{4})\rrbracket=\llbracket\llbracket\llbracket\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}(I(a_{1})),I(a_{2})\rrbracket,I(a_{3})\rrbracket,I(a_{4})\rrbracket\\
\end{split}

is, up to signs,

\begin{split}&\pm\left(\left(\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}(I(a_{1}))\circ I(a_{2})\right)\circ I(a_{3})\right)\circ I(a_{4})\pm I(a_{4})\circ\left(\left(\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}(I(a_{1}))\circ I(a_{2})\right)\circ I(a_{3})\right)\\
&\pm\left(I(a_{3})\circ\left(\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}(I(a_{1}))\circ I(a_{2})\right)\right)\circ I(a_{4})\pm\cancel{I(a_{4})\circ\left(I(a_{3})\circ\left(\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}(I(a_{1}))\circ I(a_{2})\right)\right)}\\
&\pm\left(\left(I(a_{2})\circ\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}(I(a_{1}))\right)\circ I(a_{3})\right)\circ I(a_{4})\pm\cancel{I(a_{4})\circ\left(\left(I(a_{2})\circ\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}(I(a_{1}))\right)\circ I(a_{3})\right)}\\
&\pm\left(\cancel{I(a_{3})\circ\left(I(a_{2})\circ\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}(I(a_{1}))\right)}\right)\circ I(a_{4})\pm I(a_{4})\circ\left(\cancel{I(a_{3})\circ\left(I(a_{2})\circ\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}(I(a_{1}))\right)}\right).\end{split}

Note that the contraction operator
\circ
used here is
not
associative. However a careful study using
\pr_{\sigma}\circ\sigma=0
of these eight terms show that they all vanish.
The four barred terms vanish immediately using
\pr_{\sigma}\circ\sigma=0
, since
I(a)=\sigma\circ(\pr_{\sigma}^{*}a)
and the respective parentheses all start with applying
\sigma
to a form with values in
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
. The first term vanishes
because all summands of
\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}I(a_{1})
are contracted with at least one of the forms
I(a_{2}),I(a_{3}),I(a_{4})
, and again
\pr_{\sigma}\circ\sigma=0
.
The second term vanishes because the only term that does not vanish in the parenthesis is of the form
\sigma(a_{1}(\pr_{\sigma}[I(a_{2})(\ldots),I(a_{3})(\ldots)],\pr_{\sigma}(\cdot),\ldots,\pr_{\sigma}(\cdot)))
, which is then vanishing in the contraction in
I(a_{4})
. Similarly, the last two terms vanish as well.
This shows that
m_{k}=0
for all
k\geq 4
, see also
[
10
]
.

Choose
\xi\in\wedge^{r}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}
(i.e. of degree
r-1
). Then

\begin{split}m_{1}(\xi)&=P\llbracket\mu_{\mathfrak{g}},I(\xi)\rrbracket=P\left(\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}(I(\xi))\right).\end{split}

For
h_{1},\ldots,h_{r+1}\in\mathfrak{h}
compute

\begin{split}P\left(\delta^{\ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi))\right)(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r+1})&=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\left(\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad}(I(\xi))(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r+1})\right)\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}(-1)^{i+1}\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\left[h_{i},\sigma(\xi(h_{1},\ldots,\widehat{i},\ldots,h_{r+1}))\right]\\
&\quad\quad+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(-1)^{i+j}\xi\left(\left[h_{i},h_{j}\right],h_{1},\ldots,\widehat{i},\ldots,\widehat{j},\ldots,h_{r+1}\right)\\
&=(\delta_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\text{Bott}}\xi)(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r+1}).\end{split}

This shows that
m_{1}=\delta_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\text{Bott}}
, and in particular that
m_{1}
does not depend on the choice of
\sigma
.

Now, let
\xi\in C^{r}(\mathfrak{h},{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}})=\wedge^{r}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}
and
\eta\in C^{s}(\mathfrak{h},{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}})
(of degrees
r-1
and
s-1
, respectively).
Then

\begin{split}m_{2}(\xi,\eta)&=P\llbracket\llbracket\mu_{\mathfrak{g}},I(\xi)\rrbracket,I(\eta)\rrbracket=P\left\llbracket\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi)),I(\eta)\right\rrbracket\in C^{r+s}(\mathfrak{h},{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}})\end{split}

Assume that
r=s=1
and choose
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}
. Then by (
5
)

(18)

\begin{split}m_{2}(\xi,\eta)(h_{1},h_{2})&=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\bigl(\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi))\left(\sigma(\eta(h_{1})),h_{2}\right)-\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi))\left(\sigma(\eta(h_{2})),h_{1}\right)-\sigma\left(\eta\left(p_{\sigma}\left(\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi))(h_{1},h_{2})\right)\right)\right)\bigr)\\
&=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\bigl([(\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{2})]-[h_{2},\cancel{\xi((p_{\sigma}\circ\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{1}))}]-\sigma(\xi(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{1}),h_{2}]))\\
&\qquad-[(\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{2}),(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{1})]+[h_{1},\cancel{\xi((p_{\sigma}\circ\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{2}))}]+\sigma(\xi(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{2}),h_{1}]))\bigr)\\
&\qquad-\eta\left(p_{\sigma}\left([h_{1},(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{2})]-[h_{2},(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{1})]\right)\right)+\cancel{\eta\left((p_{\sigma}\circ\sigma\circ\xi)[h_{1},h_{2}]\right)}\\
&=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}[(\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{2})]-\xi(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{1}),h_{2}])\\
&\qquad-\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}[(\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{2}),(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{1})]+\xi(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{2}),h_{1}])\\
&\qquad-\eta\left(p_{\sigma}\left([h_{1},(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{2})]-[h_{2},(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{1})]\right)\right).\end{split}

In particular

(19)

\begin{split}m_{2}(\xi,\xi)(h_{1},h_{2})=2\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}[(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{2})]-2\xi\left(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{1}),h_{2}]\right)-2\xi\left(p_{\sigma}[h_{1},(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{2})]\right).\end{split}

Last, the trinary bracket is worked out. Let
\xi,\eta,\nu\in C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{h};{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}})[1]
.
Then

\begin{split}m_{3}(\xi,\eta,\nu)=P\llbracket\llbracket\llbracket\mu_{\mathfrak{g}},I(\xi)\rrbracket,I(\eta)\rrbracket,I(\nu)\rrbracket=P\llbracket\llbracket\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi)),I(\eta)\rrbracket,I(\nu)\rrbracket.\end{split}

Assume that
|\xi|=|\eta|=|\nu|=0
and compute for
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}

(20)

\begin{split}&m_{3}(\xi,\eta,\nu)(h_{1},h_{2})=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\left(\llbracket\llbracket\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi)),I(\eta)\rrbracket,I(\nu)\rrbracket(h_{1},h_{2})\right)\\
&\,=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\left(\llbracket\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi)),I(\eta)\rrbracket((\sigma\circ\nu)(h_{1}),h_{2})-\llbracket\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi)),I(\eta)\rrbracket((\sigma\circ\nu)(h_{2}),h_{1})\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad-(\nu\circ p_{\sigma})\left(\llbracket\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi)),I(\eta)\rrbracket(h_{1},h_{2})\right).\end{split}

Easy computations give that this is

\begin{split}&\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\left(\llbracket\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi)),I(\eta)\rrbracket((\sigma\circ\nu)(h_{1}),h_{2})-\llbracket\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi)),I(\eta)\rrbracket((\sigma\circ\nu)(h_{2}),h_{1})\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad-(\nu\circ p_{\sigma})\left(\llbracket\delta^{\rm ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(I(\xi)),I(\eta)\rrbracket(h_{1},h_{2})\right)\\
&\,=-\xi\left(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\nu)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{2})]\right)-\eta\left(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\nu)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{2})]\right)-\xi\left(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\nu)(h_{2})]\right)\\
&\qquad-\eta\left(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\nu)(h_{2})]\right)-\nu\left(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{2})]\right)-\nu\left(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\eta)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{2})]\right).\end{split}

In particular,

m_{3}(\xi,\xi,\xi)(h_{1},h_{2})=-6\xi\left(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\xi)(h_{2})]\right)

for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}
.

The above are the formulas of
m_{2}
and
m_{3}
that are needed for the proof of the following proposition.

Proposition 3.5

(
[
41
,
10
]
)
.

Given a
k
-dimensional Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
and a splitting
\sigma\colon\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}\to\mathfrak{g}
of (
16
), there is a bijection between

(1)

Maurer-Cartan elements
\phi
(of degree
0
) of the
L_{\infty}[1]
-algebra
(C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{h};{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}})[1],m_{1},m_{2}^{\sigma},m_{3}^{\sigma})
and

(2)

k
-dimensional Lie subalgebras
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}\oplus\sigma({\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}})
,

given by the correspondence
\phi\mapsto\grap(\sigma\circ\phi)
.

Proof.

Consider
\phi\in\wedge^{1}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}
. Then

\grap(\sigma\circ\phi)=\{u+(\sigma\circ\phi)(u)\ |\ u\in\mathfrak{h}\}
is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
if and only if
[\grap(\sigma\circ\phi),\grap(\sigma\circ\phi)]\subset\grap(\sigma\circ\phi)
. Equivalently, this means that for every
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}
:

\displaystyle[h_{1}+(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{1}),h_{2}+(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{2})]

\displaystyle=[h_{1},h_{2}]+[h_{1},(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{2})]+[(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{1}),h_{2}]+[(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{2})]\in\grap(\sigma\circ\phi).

This is equivalent to

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\left([h_{1},(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{2})]+[(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{1}),h_{2}]+[(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{2})]\right)

\displaystyle=\phi\Bigl([h_{1},h_{2}]+p_{\sigma}\left([h_{1},(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{2})]+[(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{1}),h_{2}]+[(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{2})]\right)\Bigr).

On the other hand consider

\displaystyle m_{1}(\phi)+\frac{1}{2}m_{2}^{\sigma}(\phi,\phi)+\frac{1}{6}m_{3}^{\sigma}(\phi,\phi,\phi).

On
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}
this is

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}[h_{1},(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{2})]-\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}[h_{2},(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{1})]-\phi[h_{1},h_{2}]

\displaystyle+\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}[(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{2})]-\phi\left(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{1}),h_{2}]\right)-\phi\left(p_{\sigma}[h_{1},(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{2})]\right)

\displaystyle-\phi\left(p_{\sigma}[(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{1}),(\sigma\circ\phi)(h_{2})]\right),

which concludes the proof.
∎

Denote by
\mathbf{\Lambda}\subset\Hom(\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g})
the space of Lie brackets on the vector space
\mathfrak{g}
and by
\mathbf{S}\subset\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
the space of
k
-dimensional Lie subalgebras of the Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
.

Definition 3.6
.

A Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is called
a rigid subalgebra
if
for each open subset
V\subseteq G
containing
e\in G
there exists an open subset
U^{V}_{\mathfrak{h}}\subseteq\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
of
\mathfrak{h}
such that for every Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{g}
with
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}\in U^{V}_{\mathfrak{h}}
, there exists
g\in V
such that
the equality
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}=\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{h}
holds.

Remark 3.7
.

Note that if
\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
is rigid, then for each
g\in G
, the Lie subalgebra
\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
is rigid as well: for
V\subseteq G
open around
e

take the neighborhood

U^{V}_{\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{h}}:=\Ad_{g}\left(U^{g^{-1}Vg}_{\mathfrak{h}}\right)\subseteq\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})

of
\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{h}
. Then for all
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}\in U^{V}_{\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{h}}
there exists
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime}\in U^{g^{-1}Vg}_{\mathfrak{h}}
such that
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}=\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime}
and so there exists
g^{\prime}\in g^{-1}Vg
such that

\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}=\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime}=\Ad_{g}\Ad_{g^{\prime}}\mathfrak{h}=\Ad_{gg^{\prime}g^{-1}}\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{h},

with
gg^{\prime}g^{-1}\in V
.
In particular, if a Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is
rigid
, then
its orbit
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{h}}
is open in
\mathbf{S}
:

\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{h}}:=\{\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{h}\mid g\in G\}\subseteq\cup_{g\in G}\left(U^{G}_{\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{h}}\cap\mathbf{S}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{h}}

shows that

\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{h}}=\cup_{g\in G}\left(U^{G}_{\Ad_{g}\mathfrak{h}}\cap\mathbf{S}\right),

which is an open subset of
\mathbf{S}
.

Theorem 3.8

(
[
4
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie subalgebra. If
H^{1}_{\rm def}(\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g})=0
, then
\mathfrak{h}
is a rigid subalgebra.

A Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
is
stable
if all nearby Lie algebra structures on
\mathfrak{g}
have a nearby Lie subalgebra isomorphic to
\mathfrak{h}
.

Definition 3.9
.

A Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
is called
a stable subalgebra
if for every neighborhood
U_{\mathfrak{h}}\subset\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
of
\mathfrak{h}
, there exists a neighborhood
V_{\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}}\subseteq\mathbf{\Lambda}
of
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
such that for every
\mu^{\prime}\in V_{\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}}
there exists
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}\in U_{\mathfrak{h}}
such that
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}\subset(\mathfrak{g},\mu^{\prime})
is a Lie subalgebra.

Theorem 3.10

(
[
4
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie subalgebra. If
H^{2}_{\rm def}(\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g})=0
, then
\mathfrak{h}
is a stable subalgebra. In addition, the space of
k
-dimensional Lie subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}
is then locally a manifold, around
\mathfrak{h}
, of dimension equal to the dimension of
Z^{1}_{\rm def}(\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g})
.

4.
Infinitesimal deformations of an ideal in a Lie algebra

Consider again Definition
3.1
and Proposition
3.2

in the case where
\mathfrak{h}\coloneqq\mathfrak{i}
happens not to be just a Lie subalgebra of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, but also an ideal of
\mathfrak{g}
. In this case the Bott representation vanishes, so
H^{1}_{\rm def}(\mathfrak{i}\subset\mathfrak{g})
equals
[\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}]^{\circ}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
, and more generally, the deformation complex of the ideal as a Lie subalgebra
is then
(C^{\bullet}_{\rm def}(\mathfrak{i}\subset\mathfrak{g}),\delta_{\mathfrak{i}}^{\rm Bott})=(C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{i};\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}),\delta_{\tr})
, with the trivial representation
\tr\colon\mathfrak{i}\to\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})
, i.e. the zero linear map. This section describes the appropriate cohomology associated to deformations of an ideal
as an ideal
and not as a mere Lie subalgebra.

4.1.
Smooth deformations and infinitesimal deformations of an ideal

A Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}
of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
comes with two natural representations:

(i)

\ad^{\mathfrak{i}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{i})
,
\ad^{\mathfrak{i}}_{x}(u)=[x,u]
for all
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and all
u\in\mathfrak{i}
, and

(ii)

\ad^{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}),\quad{\ad}^{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}_{x}(\overline{y})=\overline{[x,y]}=[\overline{x},\overline{y}]
for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
.

Consider the associated
\Hom
-representation on
\Hom(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})=\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
:

\ad^{\Hom}\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\Hom(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})\to\Hom(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}),\quad\ad^{\Hom}_{x}(\phi)(u)=\ad^{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}_{x}(\phi(u))-\phi(\ad^{\mathfrak{i}}_{x}(u)),

for all
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and all
u\in\mathfrak{i}
.
Consider the corresponding Chevalley-Eilenberg complex
C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})\coloneqq\bigwedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
, with
\delta_{\mathfrak{g}\rhd\mathfrak{i}}^{\Hom}:=\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ad^{\Hom}}
. The obtained cohomology is written
H^{\bullet}_{\delta_{\mathfrak{g}\rhd\mathfrak{i}}^{\Hom}}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})=:H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g})
for simplicity. Given
f\in C^{k}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})
, the Chevalley-Eilenberg differential is given explicitly by the following formula:

(21)

\begin{split}\delta_{\mathfrak{g}\rhd\mathfrak{i}}^{\Hom}(f)(x_{1},\dots,x_{k+1},u)&=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}(-1)^{i+1}\ad^{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}_{x_{i}}(f(x_{1},\dots,\widehat{x_{i}},\dots,x_{k+1},u))\\
&+\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}(-1)^{i}f(x_{1},\dots,\widehat{x_{i}},\dots,x_{k+1},[x_{i},u])\\
&+\sum_{i<j}(-1)^{i+j}f([x_{i},x_{j}],x_{1},\dots,\widehat{x_{i}},\dots,\widehat{x_{j}},\dots,x_{k+1},u)\end{split}

for
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k+1}\in\mathfrak{g}
and
u\in\mathfrak{i}
.

Definition 4.1
.

A smooth deformation
(\mathfrak{i}_{t})_{t\in I}
of an ideal
\mathfrak{i}
inside
\mathfrak{g}
is a smooth curve
\tilde{\mathfrak{i}}\colon[0,1]\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
such that
\mathfrak{i}_{0}=\mathfrak{i}
and
\mathfrak{i}_{t}\coloneqq\tilde{\mathfrak{i}}(t)
is an ideal of
\mathfrak{g}
for all
t\in I\coloneqq[0,1]
.

Because ideals are
\Ad
-invariant,
the equivalence relation in Definition
3.1
would here become trivial: in the sense of this definition, two equivalent smooth deformations of an ideal as an ideal (not only as a Lie subalgebra) would be equal. Accordingly, two deformations of an ideal are
equivalent
if they are equal – there is no room here for a more permissive relation of equivalence of ideals.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra and let
\mathfrak{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
be an ideal. If
(\mathfrak{i}_{t})_{t\in I}
is a smooth deformation of the ideal
\mathfrak{i}
inside
\mathfrak{g}
, then

(22)

\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\mathfrak{i}_{t}\in T_{\mathfrak{i}}\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})

is a
0
-cocycle in
C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})
.

Definition 4.3
.

Because of this result, cohomology classes in
H^{0}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})
are called
infinitesimal deformations
of
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
. An infinitesimal deformation of the form
\left[\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\mathfrak{i}_{t}\right]\in H^{0}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})
with a deformation
(\mathfrak{i}_{t})_{t\in I}
of the ideal
\mathfrak{i}
inside
\mathfrak{g}
is called the
deformation class
associated to
(\mathfrak{i}_{t})_{t\in I}
.

Instead of the term
deformation class
, the expression
deformation cocycle
associated to
(\mathfrak{i}_{t})_{t\in I}
is used here as well, since
H^{0}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})=Z^{0}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})
.

This paper shows later on that not every infinitesimal deformation of
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
may be regarded as a deformation class of some
(\mathfrak{i}_{t})_{t\in I}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

First recall that
\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\mathfrak{i}_{t}
is a linear map from
\mathfrak{i}
to
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
using the canonical identification
T_{\mathfrak{i}}\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\simeq\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
(see Appendix
A.1
). In addition identify
\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}{\mathfrak{i}_{t}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\alpha(t)\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{i}}
, where the curve
4
4

4

\alpha
might be defined on a small neighborhood of
0
only.

\alpha\colon I\ni t\mapsto\alpha(t)\in\GL(\mathfrak{g})
is such that
\alpha(t)(\mathfrak{i})=\mathfrak{i}_{t}
for each
t\in I
, and where
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
is the canonical projection.

For each
t\in I
the map
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}_{t}}
has kernel
\mathfrak{i}_{t}
and factors hence to an isomorphism
\overline{\alpha(t)}\colon\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}_{t}
such that

(23)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}_{t}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha(t)}}

commutes.
The fact that
\mathfrak{i}_{t}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is an ideal for each
t\in I
means that
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{i}_{t}]\subset\mathfrak{i}_{t}
. Consider the smooth map
\sigma\colon I\to\Hom(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})
defined, for any
t\in I
,
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and
u\in\mathfrak{i}
, by

\sigma(t)(x,u)=\overline{\alpha(t)}^{-1}\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}_{t}}([x,\alpha(t)(u)])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{commutative diagram in the proof of the cocycle in Lie subalgebras}}}{{=}}\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/{\mathfrak{i}}}\circ{\alpha(t)}^{-1}([x,\alpha(t)(u)]).

\sigma
vanishes at
t\in I
if and only if
\mathfrak{i}_{t}
is an ideal.
Differentiating with respect to
t
the expression
\sigma=0
yields

(24)

0=\bigg(\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\sigma(t)\bigg)(x,u)=-\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\bigg(\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}{\alpha(t)}\bigg)\big([x,u]\big)+\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\bigg(\bigg[x,\bigg(\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}{\alpha(t)}\bigg)(u)\bigg]\bigg),

for all
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and
u\in\mathfrak{i}
.
This is the cocycle condition for the linear map
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}{\alpha(t)}\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{i}}\colon\mathfrak{i}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
in
C^{0}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})
.
∎

4.2.
Relation with other deformation cohomologies induced by ideals in Lie algebras

This section compares the deformation cohomology and deformation classes of ideals with several deformation cohomology and deformation classes associated to ideals in Lie algebras:

(1)

The ideal
\mathfrak{i}
of
\mathfrak{g}
is a subrepresentation of the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{g}
, which can be deformed as a subrepresentation, i.e. as a morphism of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{i})
, where
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{i})
is the Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})
consisting of all the endomorphisms of
\mathfrak{g}
preserving the vector subspace
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
.

(2)

The ideal
\mathfrak{i}
of
\mathfrak{g}
defines the morphism
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
of Lie algebras, which can be deformed as such a morphism, see Remark
3.3
.

(3)

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
defined by
\mathfrak{i}
can be deformed as a Lie algebra.

(4)

This section considers as well Nijenhuis and Richardson’s deformation class
[
34
]
associated to the ideal
\mathfrak{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
.

(5)

Finally, the deformation class of
\mathfrak{i}
as a mere
Lie subalgebra
of
\mathfrak{g}
can be considered.

For the convenience of the reader, the adjoint action of
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
on itself is written
\overline{\ad}
in this section. The adjoint action of
\mathfrak{g}
on itself is simply written
\ad
. It is easy to see that
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}^{*}\overline{\ad}=\ad^{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}
.

4.2.1.
Relation with the deformation cohomology of the ideal
\mathfrak{i}
as a subrepresentation of the adjoint of
\mathfrak{g}

Denote by
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{i})
the Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})
consisting of all the endomorphisms of
\mathfrak{g}
preserving the vector subspace
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
. Since the adjoint representation
\ad^{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})
of
\mathfrak{g}
preserves
\mathfrak{i}
, it has image in
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{i})
.
The natural inclusion
i_{\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})}\colon\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{i})\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})
induces the following linear map for all
k\geq 0
:

(i_{\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})})_{*}\colon C^{k}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{i}))\hookrightarrow\underbrace{C^{k}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g}))}_{C_{\defor}^{k}(\ad^{\mathfrak{g}})},\quad(i_{\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})})_{*}(\phi)(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=i_{\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})}(\phi(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})).

The representation in
C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g}))
, the deformation complex of the Lie algebra morphism
\ad^{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})
, is
