[\eta]\in H^{0}(\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g})
that is not a deformation class – in other words, there exists no smooth deformation
(\mathfrak{i}_{t})_{t\in I}
of the ideal
\mathfrak{i}
inside
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}\mathfrak{i}_{t}=\eta
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{i}^{c}
a complement of
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
and let
(\phi_{t})_{t\in I}\in\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{c}
be a smooth family of linear maps starting at the zero map. Then
\mathfrak{i}_{t}\coloneqq\Graph(\phi_{t})
is a smooth deformation of the vector space
\mathfrak{i}\coloneqq\mathfrak{i}_{0}
. Consider the Taylor expansion of
\phi_{t}
around
t=0
:

\phi_{t}\simeq t\eta+t^{2}\omega+\mathbf{O}(t^{3})

with
\eta,\omega\in\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{i}^{c}}
.
The following shows that the condition of
\Graph(\phi_{t})
being an ideal in
\mathfrak{g}
for all
t
forces the Kuranishi map to vanish on
\eta=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}\phi_{t}
. Let
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and
u\in\mathfrak{i}
, then

\displaystyle[x,u+\phi_{t}(u)]

\displaystyle=[x,u]+t[x,\eta(u)]+t^{2}[x,\omega(u)]+\mathbf{O}(t^{3})\in\Graph(\phi_{t})

for all
t

induces

t\pr_{\mathfrak{i}^{c}}([x,\eta(u)])+t^{2}(\pr_{\mathfrak{i}^{c}}[x,\omega(u)])=t\eta([x,u])+t^{2}\omega([x,u])+t^{2}\eta(\pr_{\mathfrak{i}}[x,\eta(u)])+\mathbf{O}(t^{3}),

for all
t
,
which is equivalent to the following two equations:

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathfrak{i}^{c}}([x,\eta(u)])-\eta([x,u])

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\pr_{\mathfrak{i}^{c}}([x,\omega(u)])-\eta(\pr_{\mathfrak{i}}[x,\eta(u)])-\omega([x,u])

\displaystyle=0.

Since
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and
u\in\mathfrak{i}
where arbitrary, the first equation
recovers the fact that
\delta_{\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm Hom}(\eta)=0
, while the second equation and (
38
) yield
m_{2}(\eta,\eta)=-2\cdot\delta_{\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm Hom}(\omega)
.
Hence

\Kur_{\mathfrak{i}\lhd{\mathfrak{g}}}([\eta])=\frac{1}{2}[m_{2}(\eta,\eta)]=\left[\delta_{\mathfrak{g}\rhd{\mathfrak{i}}}^{\Hom}(-\omega)\right]=0.

This shows that the Kuranishi map vanishes on deformation classes.
∎

Example 6.3

(The case
H^{2}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i};\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})=0
)
.

Recall that Proposition
4.5
shows that the cochain map

\Pi_{\wedge}\colon C^{\bullet+1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})\twoheadrightarrow C_{\wedge}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}),\quad\phi\mapsto\phi|_{\wedge^{k}\mathfrak{g}\wedge\mathfrak{i}}

sends the deformation cocycle associated to a deformation
(\pi^{t}_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}})_{t\in I}
of the projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
to the deformation cocycle defined by the deformation of the ideal
\mathfrak{i}
defined (locally) by the kernels
\left(\Ker(\pi^{t}_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}})\right)_{t\in I}
.

Combining the assumption
H^{2}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i};\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})=0
(e.g. when
\mathfrak{i}=\text{Rad}(\mathfrak{g})
or
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
is semi-simple) and (
33
)
shows that

H^{0}(\Pi_{\wedge})\colon H^{1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})\to H^{0}_{\wedge}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})=H^{0}(\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g})

is surjective. This implies that
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
is (topologically) rigid, see Definition
6.12
and Theorem
6.14

below.

In particular every deformation class of the ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
is the image under
H^{0}(\Pi_{\wedge})
of a cohomology class in the deformation cohomology of the canonical projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
.

When
H^{2}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i};\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})=0
in fact every
smooth
deformation of the ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd{\mathfrak{g}}
arises as the kernel of a smooth deformation of the canonical projection. To see this, use the fact that
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}})
is (geometrically) rigid and so every smooth deformation of the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
is (geometrically) equivalent to the trivial one (see
[
34
]
, or
[
5
, Section 4.1]
). Let
(\mathfrak{i}_{t})_{t\in I}
be a smooth deformation of the ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
. At each time
t\in I
, the ideal
\mathfrak{i}_{t}
defines the canonical projections
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}_{t}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/{\mathfrak{i}_{t}}
. The Lie algebra structures on
\mathfrak{g}/{\mathfrak{i}_{t}}
,
t\in I
, are then transferred to Lie algebras
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}^{t})
via a smooth family of linear isomorphisms denoted by
\alpha_{t}\colon\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}_{t}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
and satisfying
\alpha_{0}=\id_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}
. Then
(\mu_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}^{t})_{t\in I}
is a smooth family of deformations of the Lie bracket
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}
induced by the transfer. Using the fact that
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
is rigid, there exists a smooth family of Lie algebra isomorphisms
\phi_{t}\colon(\mathfrak{g}/{\mathfrak{i}},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}^{t})\to(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}})
, with
\phi_{0}=\id_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}
. Then the maps

\pi^{t}_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\coloneqq\phi_{t}\circ\alpha_{t}\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}_{t}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i},

for all
t\in I
, define a smooth deformation of the canonical projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}
, such that
\Ker(\pi^{t}_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}})=\mathfrak{i}_{t}
for all
t\in I
.

The following example illustrates once more how the deformation theory of an ideal is
not
its deformation theory as a Lie subalgebra.

Example 6.4

(Obstructed as an ideal
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\Longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\Longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\Longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\Longrightarrow}}}
obstructed as Lie subalgebra)
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R})\eqqcolon\mathfrak{g}
be the 3-dimensional Heisenberg algebra, in other words the Lie algebra of
(3\times 3)
-strictly upper triangular matrices. The center
\Cent(\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))\eqqcolon\mathfrak{i}
of
\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
is the
1
-dimensional ideal generated by the basis vector:

\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}

Furthermore, consider the complement
\mathfrak{i}^{c}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, which is generated by the other two canonical basis vectors:

\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

The inclusions
[\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R}),\Cent(\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))]=0
and
[\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R}),\mathfrak{i}^{c}]\subset\Cent(\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
imply that any
0
-cochain is a
0
-cocycle: for any
\eta\in\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
and any
x\in\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
and
u\in\mathfrak{i}=\Cent(\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
:

\delta_{\mathfrak{i}\rhd\mathfrak{g}}^{\Hom}(\eta)(x,u)=\pr_{\mathfrak{i}^{c}}(\underbrace{[x,\eta(u)]}_{\in\mathfrak{i}})-\eta(\underbrace{[x,u]}_{=0})=0.

The Kuranishi map is hence given here by

\Kur_{\mathfrak{i}\lhd{\mathfrak{g}}}\colon C^{0}(\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g})\to Z^{1}(\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}),\quad\Kur_{\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2}m_{2}(\eta,\eta)

with

m_{2}(\eta,\eta)(x,u)=-2\eta\left(\pr_{\mathfrak{i}}[x,\eta(u)]\right)=-2\eta\left([x,\eta(u)]\right)

for all
x\in\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
and all
u\in\mathfrak{i}
.

A linear map
\phi\colon\mathfrak{i}\to\mathfrak{i}^{c}
is uniquely defined by

\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\mapsto}}\begin{pmatrix}0&\alpha&0\\
0&0&\beta\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}

with
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.
It is a
0
-cocycle, but the
1
-cocycle
\Kur_{\mathfrak{i}\lhd{\mathfrak{g}}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}m_{2}(\phi,\phi)
does not vanish (unless
\phi=0
) since it does

\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&x&0\\
0&0&y\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}\right)\mapsto-\begin{pmatrix}0&\alpha^{2}y-\alpha\beta x&0\\
0&0&\alpha\beta y-\beta^{2}x\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore, the deformation problem of the ideal
\Cent(\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))\lhd\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
is obstructed: the ideal
\mathfrak{i}=\Cent(\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))

of
\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
admits no smooth deformation. On the other hand, since
\mathfrak{i}=\Cent(\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
has dimension
1
, any deformation of the vector subspace
\mathfrak{i}
is a deformation of
\mathfrak{i}
as a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.
Hence, the deformation problem of the Lie subalgebra
\Cent(\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))\subseteq\mathfrak{h}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
is unobstructed.

Denote by
\mathbf{S}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
the subset of
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
consisting of the space of
k
-dimensional Lie subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}
. A
k
-dimensional Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
is a point in
\mathbf{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\subset\mathbf{S}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
. The local geometry of
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
as a point in the bigger space
\mathbf{S}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
and as a point in the smaller
\mathbf{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
is hence different in general.

6.2.
Rigidity of ideals in Lie algebras

This section is inspired from the study in
[
4
]
of the rigidity of Lie algebras, Lie algebra morphisms and Lie subalgebras. Similar techniques are developed to obtain a rigidity result for Lie ideals.

Let
(E\to M,G)
be a
G
-vector bundle
, i.e. a vector bundle
E\to M
, together with a Lie group
G
acting on it by vector bundle automorphisms. That is, the smooth Lie group action

\alpha\colon G\times E\to E,\quad\alpha(g,e)\eqqcolon ge\eqqcolon\alpha_{g}(e)

restricts to a Lie group action on the zero section
M
, denoted by
\alpha^{\res}\colon G\times M\to M
such that, for all
g\in G
, the map
\alpha_{g}\colon E\to E
is a vector bundle automorphism over
\alpha^{\res}_{g}\colon M\to M
.

Remark 6.5
.

The restriction
T_{0^{E}}E
of the tangent space of
E\to M
along its zero section is canonically isomorphic as a vector bundle over
M
to
TM\oplus E
, via the isomorphism

TM\oplus E\to T_{0^{E}}E,\qquad(v_{m},e_{m})\mapsto T_{m}0^{E}(v_{m})+\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}te_{m}

In other words,
the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{0^{E}}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tq}

with the inclusion
E\hookrightarrow T_{0^{E}}E
,
e\to\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}te
,
is canonically split by the tangent of the zero section
T0^{E}\colon TM\to T_{0^{E}}E
.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
(E\to M,G)
be a
G
-vector bundle. A section
\sigma\colon M\to E
is called
G-equivariant
if
\sigma(gx)=g\sigma(x)
for any
g\in G
and
x\in M
.
The
vertical tangent map

T_{x}^{\ver}\sigma\colon T_{x}M\to E_{x}

of
\sigma\colon M\to E
at a zero
x\in\sigma^{-1}(0)\coloneqq\{x\in M\ |\ \sigma(x)=0^{E}_{x}\}
is the map
T_{x}^{\ver}\sigma\colon T_{x}M\to E_{x}

defined as the composition of
T_{x}\sigma\colon T_{x}M\to T_{0^{E}_{x}}E\simeq T_{x}M\oplus E_{x}
, followed by the projection onto
E_{x}
.
A zero
x\in M
of
\sigma
is called
non-degenerate
if the sequence

(46)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{x}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\alpha^{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{x}^{\ver}\sigma}

is exact,
where
\alpha^{x}\colon G\to M
is the orbit map of
\alpha^{\res}
at
x\in M
.

The following proposition on the openness of orbits is proved in
[
4
]
.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
(E\to M,G)
be a
G
-vector bundle, let
\sigma\colon M\to E
be a
G
-equivariant section and assume that
x\in\sigma^{-1}(0)
is nondegenerate. Then there is an open neighborhood
U
of
x
and a smooth map
h\colon U\to G
such that for all
y\in U
with
\sigma(y)=0
the equality
h(y)x=y
holds. In particular, the orbit of
x
under the
G
-action and the zero set of
\sigma
coincide in an open neighborhood of
x
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra and let
\mathfrak{i}
be an ideal of
\mathfrak{g}
of dimension
k
.
Consider the smooth vector bundle
E\to M
, with base manifold
M\coloneqq\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
and fiber over
W\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
given by
E_{W}\coloneqq\{W\}\times\Hom(\mathfrak{g},W^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/W)
. Let
\operatorname{Taut}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
be the tautological vector bundle, i.e.
\operatorname{Taut}_{k}
is the rank
k
vector subbundle of
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g}
given by
\operatorname{Taut}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\arrowvert_{\{W\}}=\{W\}\times W
. Then
E\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
is the smooth vector bundle

(47)

E=(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})\otimes(\operatorname{Taut}_{k}(\mathfrak{g}))^{*}\otimes\frac{\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g}}{\operatorname{Taut}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})}\longrightarrow\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g}).

The dual vector bundle
\Taut_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{g})
is the quotient of
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
by its smooth vector subbundle

(\Taut_{k}(\mathfrak{g}))^{\circ}=\cup_{W\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})}\{W\}\times\{l\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\mid l(w)=0\text{ for all }w\in W\}.

The vector bundle
E\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
comes as a consequence with the smooth vector bundle projection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E}}

sending
(W,\phi)\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})
to

(W,\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}\circ\phi\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes W})\in E\arrowvert_{W},

see Appendix
A.2
, where local trivialisations of
E
are given.

The section

(48)

\sigma\colon M\to E,\quad W\mapsto\left(W,\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}\circ\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes W}\right)

is smooth since it can be written as
P\circ\tilde{\sigma}\colon\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\to E
, with the constant section

\tilde{\sigma}\colon\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}),\qquad W\mapsto(W,\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}).

The zero set
\sigma^{-1}(0)
of
\sigma
is exactly the space
\bm{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\subset\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Remark 6.8
.

Note that this construction is independent of
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfying the Jacobi identity (or being skew-symmetric). In fact, for each
\nu\in\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\simeq\Hom(\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g})
the constant section

\tilde{\nu}\colon\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}),\qquad W\mapsto(W,\nu)

projects as above to a smooth section

\Sigma(\nu)\colon\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\to E,\qquad W\mapsto\left(W,\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}\circ\nu\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes W}\right)

such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma(\nu)}

commutes.
The map

\Sigma\colon\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\Gamma(E)

is then an
\mathbb{R}
-linear map.

The following computes explicitly the vertical differential
T_{\mathfrak{i}}^{\ver}\sigma\colon T_{\mathfrak{i}}\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\to E_{\mathfrak{i}}
.
The following lemma is useful for this.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\pi\colon E\to M
be a smooth vector bundle with a vector bundle isomorphism
\phi\colon E\to M\times E\arrowvert_{p}

for some
p\in M
, such that
\phi\arrowvert_{p}\colon E\arrowvert_{p}\to\{p\}\times E\arrowvert_{p}
is the identity. If
p
is a zero of a smooth section
\sigma\colon M\to E
, then

T_{p}^{\ver}\sigma=T_{p}^{\ver}(\phi\circ\sigma)\colon T_{p}M\to E\arrowvert_{p},

and so

T_{p}(\phi\circ\sigma)(v_{p})=\left(v_{p},0^{E}_{p},T_{p}^{\ver}\sigma(v_{p})\right)\in T_{p}M\times\left\{0^{E}_{p}\right\}\times E\arrowvert_{p}=T_{p}M\times T_{0^{E}_{p}}(E\arrowvert_{p})

for all
v_{p}\in T_{p}M
.

Proof.

By definition,
T_{p}^{\ver}\sigma\colon T_{p}M\to E\arrowvert_{p}
sends
v_{p}\in T_{p}M
to
T_{p}\sigma v_{p}-T_{p}0^{E}v_{p}\in E\arrowvert_{p}=T^{\pi}_{0^{E}_{p}}E
. As a consequence,

\begin{split}T^{\ver}_{p}(\phi\circ\sigma)(v_{p})&=T_{p}(\phi\circ\sigma)(v_{p})-T_{p}0^{M\times E\arrowvert_{p}}(v_{p})=T_{0^{E}_{p}}\phi(T_{p}\sigma v_{p})-T_{0^{E}_{p}}\phi(T_{p}0^{E}v_{p})\\
&=T_{0^{E}_{p}}\phi(T_{p}\sigma v_{p}-T_{p}0^{E}v_{p})=T_{0^{E}_{p}}\phi\left(T^{\ver}_{p}\sigma v_{p}\right)\end{split}

for all
v_{p}\in T_{p}M
. Choose
e_{p}\in E\arrowvert_{p}
and consider the corresponding
vertical vector

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}te_{p}\in T_{0^{E}_{p}}E.

Then

T_{0^{E}_{p}}\phi\left(\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}te_{p}\right)=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}t\phi(e_{p})=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}te_{p}

since
\phi\arrowvert_{p}\colon E\arrowvert_{p}\to\{p\}\times E\arrowvert_{p}
is the identity map. This shows that

T^{\ver}_{p}\sigma v_{p}=T_{0^{E}_{p}}\phi\left(T^{\ver}_{p}\sigma v_{p}\right)=T^{\ver}_{p}(\phi\circ\sigma)(v_{p})

for all
v_{p}\in T_{p}M
.

The second statement then follows immediately.
∎

The last lemma shows that the map
T_{\mathfrak{i}}^{\ver}\sigma\colon T_{\mathfrak{i}}\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\to E_{\mathfrak{i}}
can be computed in a chart
U:=U^{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{c}}\simeq\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{c}
given by a choice of complement
\mathfrak{i}^{c}
for
\mathfrak{i}
in
\mathfrak{g}
, since
it induces a trivialisation

E\arrowvert_{U^{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{c}}}\to U^{\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{i}^{c}}\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})

that is the identity on
E\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{i}}
. By (
52
),
the image under the isomorphism
\Phi^{-1}\colon E\arrowvert_{U}\to U\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})

of
\sigma\arrowvert_{U}\colon U\to E\arrowvert_{U}

is given by

\tilde{\sigma}:=\Phi^{-1}\circ\sigma\colon U\to U\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}),\qquad\tilde{\sigma}(\grap(\phi))=\left(\phi,\mu_{\phi}\right)

for all
\phi\in\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{c}
,
with
\mu_{\phi}\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
given by

\mu_{\phi}(x,u)=\pr_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}[x,u+\phi(u)]-(\pr_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\phi\circ\pr_{\mathfrak{i}})[x,u+\phi(u)]

for all
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and all
u\in\mathfrak{i}
.

Take a smooth curve
\phi\colon I\to U
with
\phi(0)=0
i.e.
\grap(\phi(0))=\mathfrak{i}
. Then as discussed in Appendix
A.1
,

\dot{\phi}(0)\in T_{\mathfrak{i}}\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}

and by the considerations above

\begin{split}T^{\ver}_{\mathfrak{i}}\sigma\left(\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}\phi(t)\right)=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}\mu_{\phi(t)}.\end{split}

On
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and
u\in\mathfrak{i}
,

\begin{split}&\left(\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}\mu_{\phi(t)}\right)(x,u)=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}\left(\pr_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}[x,u+\phi(t)(u)]-(\pr_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\phi(t)\circ\pr_{\mathfrak{i}})[x,u+\phi(t)(u)]\right)\\
&=\pr_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\left[x,\dot{\phi}(0)(u)\right]-\left(\pr_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\dot{\phi}(0)\circ\pr_{\mathfrak{i}}\right)\left[x,u+\phi(0)(u)\right]-(\pr_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\phi(0)\circ\pr_{\mathfrak{i}})\left[x,u+\dot{\phi}(0)(u)\right]\\
&=\pr_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\left[x,\dot{\phi}(0)(u)\right]-\left(\pr_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\dot{\phi}(0)\circ\pr_{\mathfrak{i}}\right)\underset{\in\mathfrak{i}}{\underbrace{\left[x,u\right]}}=\pr_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\left[x,\dot{\phi}(0)(u)\right]-(\pr_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\dot{\phi}(0))\left[x,u\right]\end{split}

since
7
7

7

In this computation
\dot{\phi}(0)
is considered an element of
\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{c}
.

\phi(0)=0
. This shows that

T^{\ver}_{\mathfrak{i}}\sigma\left(\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}\phi(t)\right)=\delta_{\mathfrak{g}\rhd{\mathfrak{i}}}^{\Hom}\left(\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}\phi(t)\right).

Since the curve
\phi\colon I\to U
was arbitrary, this shows that

T^{\ver}_{\mathfrak{i}}\sigma=\delta_{\mathfrak{g}\rhd{\mathfrak{i}}}^{\Hom}\colon\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}.

Let
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
be the Lie group of Lie algebra automorphisms of
(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
and consider the (left) linear Lie group action

\tilde{\alpha}\colon\Aut(\mathfrak{g})\times(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}))\to(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}))

defined by

(\Psi,(W,\omega)\mapsto(\Psi(W),\Psi\circ\omega\circ(\Psi^{-1},\Psi^{-1})),

where
\omega\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
is seen as an element of
\Hom(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g})
. The action
\tilde{\alpha}
is linear over the canonical Lie group action
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})\times\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
,
(\Psi,W)\mapsto\Psi(W)
.
By the following proposition, there is a unique Lie group action

\alpha\colon\Aut(\mathfrak{g})\times E\to E

such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Aut(\mathfrak{g})\times(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Aut(\mathfrak{g})\times E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Aut(\mathfrak{g})}\times P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

commutes. The Lie group action
\alpha
is explicitly
defined by

\big(\Psi,(W,\phi_{W})\big)\mapsto\big(\Psi(W),\Psi\phi_{W}\big)\in\{\Psi(W)\}\times\Hom\big(\mathfrak{g},(\Psi(W))^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/{\Psi(W)}\big),

where the second component is given on
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and
w\in W
by:

(49)

\Psi\phi_{W}(x,\Psi(w))\coloneqq\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\Psi(W)}\circ\Psi\big((\underbrace{\phi_{W}(\Psi^{-1}(x),w)}_{\in\mathfrak{g}/W})^{\text{lift}}\big),

where for any
y\in\mathfrak{g}/W
, the element
y^{\text{lift}}
of
\mathfrak{g}
is an arbitrary choice of element of
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}\left(y^{\text{lift}}\right)=y
.

Proposition 6.10
.

In the situation above, the pair
(E\to M,\Aut(\mathfrak{g}))
is an
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
-vector bundle and its section
\sigma
defined in (
48
) is
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
-equivariant.

This follows from the following lemma, the proof of which is straightforward and left to the reader.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
F\to M
be a smooth vector bundle over a smooth manifold
M
, let
F_{0}\subset F
be a vector subbundle over
M
, and let
G
be a Lie group.
Let
P\colon F\to F/F_{0}
be the canonical epimorphism of vector bundles over the identity on
M
.
Assume that

\tilde{\alpha}\colon G\times F\to F,\qquad\alpha_{0}\colon G\times M\to M

are smooth (left) Lie group actions, such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}

is a vector bundle homomorphism.

(1)

If
\tilde{\alpha}_{g}(F_{0})=F_{0}
for all
g\in G
, then
\tilde{\alpha}
quotients to a smooth Lie group action

\alpha\colon G\times F/F_{0}\to F/F_{0},\qquad\alpha(g,f_{p}+F_{0}(p))\mapsto\tilde{\alpha}(g,f_{p})+F_{0}(gp)

for all
g\in G
,
p\in M
and
f_{p}\in F(p)
, i.e. such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\times F/F_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F/F_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\times F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\times M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{G}\times P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}

commutes.

(2)

Consider a smooth
G
-equivariant section
\tilde{\sigma}\colon M\to F
. Then
\tilde{\sigma}
quotients
to a smooth
G
-equivariant section
\sigma:=P\circ\tilde{\sigma}\colon M\to F/F_{0}
, i.e. such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\times F/F_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F/F_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\times F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\times M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{G}\times P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{G}\times\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{G}\times\tilde{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\sigma}}

commutes.

Proof of Proposition
6.10
.

The vector bundle
E\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
is the quotient of
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})
by the kernel

(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})\otimes\Taut_{k}(\mathfrak{g})^{\circ}\otimes(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g})\,+\,(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})\otimes(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})\otimes\Taut_{k}(\mathfrak{g}).

For
\alpha
to be defined, it suffices hence to show that this vector subbundle of
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})
is invariant under the action
\tilde{\alpha}
.
Take
(W,\omega)
in the first summand, i.e.
W\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\omega\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
does
\omega(x,w)=0
for all
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and all
w\in W
.
Then for all
\Psi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
, for all
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and all
w\in W

(\Psi\circ\omega)\left(\Psi^{-1}(x),\Psi^{-1}(\Psi(w))\right)=\Psi\left(\omega\left(\Psi^{-1}(x),w\right)\right)=0,

which shows that

\Psi\cdot(W,\omega)=\left(\Psi(W),\Psi\circ\omega\circ(\Psi^{-1},\Psi^{-1})\right)

lies again in
(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})\otimes\Taut_{k}(\mathfrak{g})^{\circ}\otimes(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g})
.
Similarly, the subspace
(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})\otimes(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})\otimes\Taut_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
is clearly preserved by the
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
-action on
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})
.

It remains therefore to check that
\tilde{\sigma}\colon\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})
is
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
-equivariant. But this is immediate since
\tilde{\sigma}
is the constant section

\tilde{\sigma}(W)=(W,\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})

for all
W\in\Gr_{k}(W)
, and

\Psi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
is by definition an isomorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
preserving
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
:

\Psi\circ\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\circ\left(\Psi^{-1},\Psi^{-1}\right)=\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}.

∎

The differential at
\Id_{\mathfrak{g}}\in\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
of the orbit map
\alpha^{\mathfrak{i}}\colon\Aut(\mathfrak{g})\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
of the action
\alpha^{\res}
is computed as follows.
First recall that

T_{\Id_{\mathfrak{g}}}(\Aut({\mathfrak{g}}))=\mathfrak{aut}(\mathfrak{g})\coloneqq\{\phi\in\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})\ |\ \phi([x,y])=[\phi(x),y]+[x,\phi(y)]\}=Z^{1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g}).

Therefore,
T_{\Id_{\mathfrak{g}}}\alpha^{\mathfrak{i}}\colon\mathfrak{aut}(\mathfrak{g})\to T_{\mathfrak{i}}\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
. Let
(\phi_{t})_{t\in I}\in\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
a smooth curve of automorphisms such that
\phi_{0}=\Id_{\mathfrak{g}}
. Then as an element of
T_{\mathfrak{i}}\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
,

\displaystyle T_{\Id_{\mathfrak{g}}}\alpha^{\mathfrak{i}}\bigg(\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\phi_{t}\bigg)=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\phi_{t}(\mathfrak{i})=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\dot{\phi}(0)\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{i}},

see the computation at the end of Appendix
A.1
.
This shows that for all
\psi\in\mathfrak{aut}(\mathfrak{g})
,
T_{\Id_{\mathfrak{g}}}\alpha^{\mathfrak{i}}(\psi)=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\psi|_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\Hom(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})\simeq T_{\mathfrak{i}}\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
.

As a summary, the sequence (
46
) reads here

\displaystyle Z^{1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{aut}(\mathfrak{g})\longrightarrow

\displaystyle T_{\mathfrak{i}}\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow E_{\mathfrak{i}}=\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}

with the first map

\psi\mapsto\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\psi\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{i}}

and the second map

\psi\mapsto\delta^{\Hom}_{\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}}\psi.

Definition 6.12
.

An ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
is called a
(topologically) rigid ideal
if the space of
k
-dimensional Lie ideals
\mathbf{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
coincides locally, in some open neighborhood of
\mathfrak{i}\in\mathbf{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\subset\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
, with the
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
-orbit of
\mathfrak{i}
. Namely,
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
is (topologically) rigid if there exists a neighborhood
U_{\mathfrak{i}^{\perp}}
of
\mathfrak{i}\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
such that every
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}\in U_{\mathfrak{i}^{\perp}}\cap\mathbf{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
belongs in the orbit of
\mathfrak{i}
:
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}=\Psi(\mathfrak{i})
, for some
\Psi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
.

Remark 6.13
.

Note that in the case of Lie subalgebras, two Lie subalgebras are considered equivalent
if they are related by an
inner automorphism
of the ambient Lie algebra. As already mentioned, in the case of an ideal, this equivalence relation becomes trivial and consequently not interesting. The following theorem shows that the definition of rigidity above is more natural in the context of Lie ideals. The current section studies the moduli space of Lie ideals under this natural action of the automorphism group of the ambient Lie algebra on the space of
k
-dimensional Lie ideals.

Theorem 6.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}\lhd(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
be a Lie ideal. If

H^{0}(\Pi)\colon H^{1}_{\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})\to H^{0}(\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}),\ [\phi]\mapsto\phi|_{\mathfrak{i}}

is surjective, then
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
is a (topologically) rigid ideal with respect to the
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
-action.

Proof.

The vector bundle
(E\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g}),\Aut(\mathfrak{g}))
is an
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
-vector bundle and the section
\sigma\colon\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\to E
is
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
-equivariant by Proposition
6.10
.
The only assumption that remains to be checked, in order to apply Proposition
6.7
, is the non-degeneracy of the zero
\mathfrak{i}
of
\sigma
.
The map
T_{\Id_{\mathfrak{g}}}\alpha^{\mathfrak{i}}\colon\psi\mapsto\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\psi|_{\mathfrak{i}}
factors as follows through the map
(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}})_{*}:Z^{1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})\to Z^{1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}),\ \psi\mapsto\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}}\circ\psi
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{0}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\Id_{\mathfrak{g}}}\alpha^{\mathfrak{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathfrak{g}\rhd\mathfrak{i}}^{\Hom}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}

with
\Pi
as in Proposition
4.5
:

\Pi\colon C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})[1]\to C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}),\ f\mapsto f|_{\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}\wedge\mathfrak{i}},

restricted at degree 0 cocycles. The above sequence is exact if
\img(\Pi)\subset C^{0}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})
is equal to
\Ker(\delta^{\Hom}_{\mathfrak{g}\rhd{\mathfrak{i}}})=Z^{0}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})
. This is exactly the surjectivity of
H^{0}(\Pi)
.
∎

The last theorem and Proposition
4.11
have together the following corollary.

Corollary 6.15
.

If
H^{2}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i};\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})=0
, then
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
is (topologically) rigid under the
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
-action.

Example 6.16
.

This shows that the radical ideal
\operatorname{Rad}(\mathfrak{g})
of a finite-dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is topologically rigid with respect to the
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
-action, since the quotient
\mathfrak{g}/\operatorname{Rad}(\mathfrak{g})
is semi-simple and so

H^{2}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{g}}{\operatorname{Rad}(\mathfrak{g})};\frac{\mathfrak{g}}{\operatorname{Rad}(\mathfrak{g})}\right)=0,

by Whitehead’s (second) lemma, see e.g.
[
19
]
.

6.3.
Stability of ideals in Lie algebras

This section is inspired from the study in
[
4
]
of the stability of Lie algebras, Lie algebra morphisms and Lie subalgebras. Similar techniques are developed to obtain a stability result for Lie ideals.

Proposition 6.17
.

(Stability of zeros
[
4
]
)
Let
E\to M
and
F\to M
be two vector bundles over the same base,
\sigma\in\Gamma(E)
and
\tau\in\Gamma(\Hom(E,F))
satisfying
\tau\circ\sigma=0
. In addition, let
x\in\sigma^{-1}(0)
and assume that the following sequence is exact:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{x}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{x}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{x}^{\ver}\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{x}}

Then the following statements hold true:

(1)

\sigma^{-1}(0)
is locally a manifold around
x
of dimension equal to
\dim\left(\Ker(T_{x}^{\ver}\sigma)\right)
.

(2)

For each open neighborhood
U
of
x
in
M
there exist
C^{0}
-open subsets
V\subseteq\Gamma(E)
and
W\subseteq\Gamma(\Hom(E,F))
around
\sigma
and
\tau
, respectively, such that
for all
\sigma^{\prime}\in V
and
\tau^{\prime}\in W
with
\tau^{\prime}\circ\sigma^{\prime}=0
, there exists
x^{\prime}\in U
such that
\sigma^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=0
.

(3)

In the situation of (2) there exists as well a
C^{1}
-open subset
V^{1}\subseteq V
around
\sigma
such that if
\sigma^{\prime}\in V^{1}
, then the zero set of
\sigma^{\prime}
is also locally a manifold around
x^{\prime}
, of the same dimension as
\sigma^{-1}(0)
.

In the following,
E\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
is the vector bundle already defined in (
47
) for the rigidity of a Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
, and
\sigma
is its section
\sigma\colon\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\to E,\ W\mapsto\sigma_{W}\in E\arrowvert_{W}
with
\sigma_{W}(x,w)=\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}([x,w])
for all
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and all
w\in W
.
The second vector bundle
\pi_{F}\colon F\to M
is defined to be the vector bundle over
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
with fiber over
W\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
defined by
F\arrowvert_{W}\coloneqq\{W\}\times\Hom(\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g};W^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/W)
, i.e.
F
is the smooth vector bundle

F=(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*})\otimes(\operatorname{Taut}_{k}(\mathfrak{g}))^{*}\otimes\frac{\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathfrak{g}}{\operatorname{Taut}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})}\longrightarrow\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g}).

Like
E
, the vector bundle
F
is a quotient vector bundle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F}}

with the smooth quotient map
P_{F}
sending
(W,\eta)
to

(W,\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}\circ\eta\arrowvert_{\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}\otimes W}).

Consider the vector bundle morphism

\tilde{\tau}\colon\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g}))\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g}))

over the identity
\id_{\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})}
, which is defined by

\tilde{\tau}(W,\phi)\mapsto(W,\delta_{\ad^{\mathfrak{g}}}\phi)

for all
W\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
and all
\phi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})
.
Recall here that
\delta_{\ad^{\mathfrak{g}}}\colon\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})\to\wedge^{\bullet+1}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})

is the differential defined in Section
4.2.1
by the representation
r\coloneqq(\ad^{\mathfrak{g}})^{*}\left(\ad^{\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})}\right)
of
\mathfrak{g}
on
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})
:

r\colon\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g}),\qquad(r_{x}(\phi))(y)=[x,\phi(y)]-\phi[x,y]

for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
and all
\phi\in\mathfrak{gl}(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
.

The smooth vector bundle morphism

\tau:=P_{F}\circ\tilde{\tau}\circ S\colon E\to F,

with
S\colon E\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})
defined as in Appendix
A.2
by a choice of inner product on
\mathfrak{g}
,
then sends
(W,\eta)\in E\arrowvert_{W}=\{W\}\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes W^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/W)
to

(W,\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}\circ(\delta_{\ad^{\mathfrak{g}}}(S_{W}(\eta))\arrowvert_{\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}\otimes W}))\in\{W\}\otimes(\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes W^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/W)=F\arrowvert_{W}.

The form
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}\circ(\delta_{\ad^{\mathfrak{g}}}(S_{W}(\eta))\arrowvert_{\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}\otimes W})=:\tau_{W}(\eta)\in\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes W^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/W
is explicitly
defined by

\displaystyle\tau_{W}(\eta)(x,y,w)=

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}([x,s_{W}(\eta(y,w))])-\eta(y,\pr_{W}[x,w])

\displaystyle-\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}([y,s_{W}(\eta(x,w))])+\eta(x,\pr_{W}[y,w])-\eta([x,y],w)

for
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
and
w\in W
.
Write again
\sigma_{W}
for the second component
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}\circ\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes W}
of the image under
\sigma\colon\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\to E
of
W\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
. Then, using the Jacobi identity for
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}

(50)

\begin{split}\tau_{W}(\sigma_{W})(x,y,w)=&\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}[x,s_{W}\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}[y,w]]-\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}[y,\pr_{W}[x,w]]\\
&-\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}[y,s_{W}\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}[x,w]]+\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}[x,\pr_{W}[y,w]]-\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}[[x,y],w]\\
=&\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}[x,\pr_{\operatorname{orth}(W)}[y,w]]-\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}[y,\pr_{W}[x,w]]\\
&-\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}[y,\pr_{\operatorname{orth}(W)}[x,w]]+\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}[x,\pr_{W}[y,w]]-\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}[[x,y],w]\\
=&\pi_{\mathfrak{g}/W}([x,[y,w]]-[y,[x,w]]-[[x,y],w])=0\end{split}

for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
and all
w\in W
.
This shows that
\tau_{W}\circ\sigma_{W}=0
for all
W\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
and so that

\tau\circ\sigma=0.

Remark 6.18
.

Each element
\eta\in\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
defines a linear map

\delta_{\eta}\colon\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}

by

\delta_{\eta}(\nu)(x_{1},x_{2},x)=\eta(x_{1},\nu(x_{2},x))-\nu(x_{2},\eta(x_{1},x))-\eta(x_{2},\nu(x_{1},x))+\nu(x_{1},\eta(x_{2},x))-\nu(\eta(x_{1},x_{2}),x)

for all
\nu\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
and
x_{1},x_{2},x\in\mathfrak{g}
, and the map

\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\Hom\left(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g},\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\right),\qquad\eta\to\delta_{\eta}

is again linear. Hence it defines a linear map

\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\Gamma\left(\Hom\left(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}),\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})\right)\right),\qquad\eta\to\widetilde{\delta_{\eta}},

where for
\psi\in\Hom\left(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g},\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\right)
, the section

\widetilde{\psi}\in\Gamma\left(\Hom\left(\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}),\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})\right)\right)

is the constant section defined by
\psi
.
The map

\mathrm{T}\colon\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\Gamma\left(\Hom(E,F)\right),\qquad\eta\mapsto P_{F}\circ\widetilde{\delta_{\eta}}\circ S

is then also
\mathbb{R}
-linear since
P_{F}\colon\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})\to F
and
S\colon E\to\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\times(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g})
are vector bundle morphisms over the identity on
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Lemma 6.19
.

Let
\Lambda\subset\Hom(\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})
be the space of Lie brackets on the vector space
\mathfrak{g}
.
Consider the map

(51)

\Theta\colon\Lambda\to\Gamma(E)\times\Gamma(\Hom(E,F)),\quad\mu\mapsto\big(\Sigma(\mu),\mathrm{T}(\mu)\big),

with
\Sigma\colon\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\Gamma(E)
the linear map defined in Remark
6.8
and
\mathrm{T}\colon\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\Gamma(F)
the linear map defined in
Remark
6.18
.
Then
\Theta
is continuous with respect to the product compact-open
C^{k}
-topology on
\Gamma(E)\times\Gamma(\Hom(E,F))
, for any
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

The maps
\Sigma\colon\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\Gamma(E)
and
\phi\colon\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\Gamma(\Hom(E,F))
are continuous as linear maps from finite dimensional Hausdorff topological vector spaces to topological vector space, see e.g.
[
42
, Lemma 1.20]
.
The natural inclusion
i\colon\Lambda\to\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
is continuous, since
\Lambda
is equipped with the subspace topology, and so
\Sigma\arrowvert_{\Lambda}
and
\mathrm{T}\arrowvert_{\Lambda}
are continuous by the following commutative diagrams.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma(E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma(\Hom(E,F))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\arrowvert_{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\arrowvert_{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}

∎

Roughly speaking, an ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
is (topologically) stable if for any Lie bracket
\mu^{\prime}

close enough
to
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
, there exists a Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}\lhd(\mathfrak{g},\mu^{\prime})

close enough
to
\mathfrak{i}\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
.
The precise definition is the following.

Definition 6.20
.

An ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
in a Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
is called a
(topologically) stable ideal
if for each neighborhood
U\subset\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
of
\mathfrak{i}
in
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
there exists a neighborhood
V\subset\Lambda
of
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
in
\Lambda
such that for every
\mu^{\prime}\in V
there exists
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}\in U
with
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}\lhd(\mathfrak{g},\mu^{\prime})
.

Theorem 6.21
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}\lhd(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
be an ideal. If
H^{1}(\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g})=0
, then
\mathfrak{i}
is a (topologically) stable ideal. Furthermore, in this case, the space of
k
-dimensional Lie ideals
\mathbf{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\subset\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
is locally around
\mathfrak{i}
, a manifold of dimension equal to
\dim\big(Z^{0}(\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g})\big)
. In particular,
Z^{0}(\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g})
is the tangent space of
\mathbf{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
at
\mathfrak{i}
and each infinitesimal deformation is a deformation class.

Proof.

Consider the map
\Theta
defined in (
51
)

\Theta\colon\Lambda\to\Gamma(E)\times\Gamma(\Hom(E,F)),\quad\mu\mapsto\big(\Sigma(\mu),\mathrm{T}(\mu)\big).

The same computation as (
50
) but with
\mu\in\Lambda
replacing
[\cdot,\,\cdot]=\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
shows that the identity
\mathrm{T}(\mu)\circ\Sigma(\mu)=0
is satisfied for all Lie brackets
\mu\in\Lambda
on
\mathfrak{g}
.

It is easy to see using Section
4.2.1
that
\tau=\mathrm{T}(\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
restricted to

E\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{i}}=\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}\longrightarrow F\arrowvert_{\mathfrak{i}}=\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}

is the linear map
\delta_{\mathfrak{g}\rhd\mathfrak{i}}^{\Hom}
.
Hence the sequence of Proposition
6.17
at the point
\mathfrak{i}\in\mathbf{I}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{0}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{2}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathfrak{g}\rhd\mathfrak{i}}^{\Hom}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathfrak{g}\rhd\mathfrak{i}}^{\Hom}}

and it is exact if and only if
H^{1}(\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g})=0
. Hence Proposition
6.17
can be applied here.

Choose an open subset
U\subseteq\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
around
\mathfrak{i}\in\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
. Then there exist
V\subseteq\Gamma(E)
and
W\subseteq\Gamma(\Hom(E,F))

C^{0}
-open around
\sigma
and
\tau
as in (2) of Proposition
6.17
.
The continuity of the map (
51
) guarantees that the pre-image
\Theta^{-1}(V\times W)
of
V\times W
under
\Theta
in the
C^{0}
compact-open topology is an open neighborhood
O_{\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}}\subset\Lambda
around
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
. Let
\mu^{\prime}\in O_{\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}}
and consider
\sigma^{\prime}\coloneqq\Sigma(\mu^{\prime})
and
\tau^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathrm{T}(\mu^{\prime})
. Recall that then
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\tau^{\prime}=0
, due to the Jacobi identity. Hence by (2) of Proposition
6.17
there exists
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}\in U
such that
\sigma^{\prime}(\mathfrak{i}^{\prime})=0
. ∎

Remark 6.22
.

Note that this result can also be deduced from
[
44
, Theorem 3.20]
: the differential graded algebra
(C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})[1],\llbracket\cdot\,,\cdot\rrbracket,0)
of Section
2.3.1
has the differential graded Lie subalgebra
C_{\mathfrak{i}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})[1]
defined in Subsection
4.2.4
. A choice of complement
\mathfrak{i}^{c}
for
\mathfrak{i}
in
\mathfrak{g}
defines splittings
\sigma_{0}\colon C^{1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})/C^{1}_{\mathfrak{i}}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})\to C^{1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})
in degree
0
and

\sigma_{1}\colon C^{2}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})/C^{2}_{\mathfrak{i}}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})\simeq C^{1}_{\wedge}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})\to C^{2}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})
in degree
1
. The Lie bracket
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
is a Maurer-Cartan element of
(C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})[1],\llbracket\cdot\,,\cdot\rrbracket,0)
that lies in
C^{2}_{\mathfrak{i}}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})
since
\mathfrak{i}
is an ideal in
(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
. By Proposition
4.8
, the cokernel complex

\left(\frac{C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})[1]}{C^{\bullet}_{\mathfrak{i}}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})[1]},-\overline{\delta_{\rm ad}}=-\overline{\llbracket\mu_{\mathfrak{g}},\cdot\rrbracket}\right)

of the inclusion
C^{\bullet}_{\mathfrak{i}}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})[1]\hookrightarrow C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})[1]
is isomorphic to
\left(C_{\wedge}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}),\delta^{\rm Hom}_{\mathfrak{g}\rhd\mathfrak{i}}\right)
. Hence, according to Theorem 3.20 in
[
44
]
, if

H^{1}_{\wedge}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})=0
,
then for every open neighborhood
V
of
0\in C^{1}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})/C^{1}_{\mathfrak{i}}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})
(hence for every open neighborhood of
\mathfrak{i}
in
\Gr_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
), there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
in
\Lambda

such that for any
\mu\in U

there exists a family
I\subseteq V
diffeomorphic to an open neighborhood of
0\in Z^{0}_{\wedge}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})=Z^{0}(\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g})
, with

\mu^{\sigma_{0}(\phi)}\in C_{\mathfrak{i}}^{2}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})
for all
\phi\in I
.
Using the splitting
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{i}\oplus\mathfrak{i}^{c}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, the bracket
\mu^{\sigma_{0}(\phi)}
is explicitly given by

\mu^{\sigma_{0}(\phi)}(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\phi&1\end{pmatrix}\mu\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\phi&1\end{pmatrix}x,\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\phi&1\end{pmatrix}y\right)

for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{i}\oplus\mathfrak{i}^{c}
. Since
\mu^{\sigma_{0}(\phi)}
lies in
C^{2}_{\mathfrak{i}}(\mathfrak{g};\mathfrak{g})
, it has
\mathfrak{i}
as an ideal. But this is equivalent to
\operatorname{graph}(-\phi)
being an ideal of
\mu
.

Hence
[
44
, Theorem 3.20]
implies that Theorem
6.21
holds with the weaker condition
H^{1}_{\wedge}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{i}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i})=0
.

Corollary 6.23
.

If
(\mathfrak{g},\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})
is a semisimple Lie algebra, then every Lie ideal
\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g}
is a (topologically) stable ideal.

Proof.

By Whitehead’s (first) lemma, see e.g.
[
19
]
, follows that
H^{1}(\mathfrak{i}\lhd\mathfrak{g})=0
and so the claim follows by Theorem
6.21
.
∎

Appendix A
Useful background on Grassmannians

This appendix collects useful structural results on Grassmann manifolds, their tangent spaces and their tautological bundles.

A.1.
Tangent spaces to the Grassmannian

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional vector space and choose
k\in\{0,\ldots,\dim V\}
. The
k
-Grassmannian of
V
is the space

\Gr_{k}(V)=\{W\subseteq V\mid W\text{ vector subspace of dimension }k\}.

For each
W\in\Gr_{k}(V)
and each choice of linear complement
W^{c}\subseteq V
of
W
in
V
, the
map

\Psi_{W,W^{c}}\colon W^{*}\otimes W^{c}\to\{W^{\prime}\in\Gr_{k}(V)\mid W^{\prime}\oplus W^{c}=V\}=:U_{W,W^{c}},\qquad\phi\mapsto\grap(\phi)

is a bijection with inverse

\Psi_{W,W^{c}}^{-1}\colon U_{W,W^{c}}\to W^{*}\otimes W^{c},\qquad W^{\prime}\mapsto\phi=\pr_{W^{c}}\arrowvert_{W^{\prime}}\circ(\pr_{W}\arrowvert_{W^{\prime}})^{-1},

where
\pr_{W^{c}}\colon V\to W^{c}
and
\pr_{W}\colon V\to W
are the linear projections defined by the splitting
V=W\oplus W^{c}
of
V
.

The set
\Gr_{k}(V)
has a unique topology and a unique smooth structure such that for each
W\in\Gr_{k}(V)
and each choice of complement
W^{c}
as above, the map
\Psi^{W,W^{c}}
is a smooth chart of
\Gr_{k}(V)
centered at
W
. The smooth manifold
\Gr_{k}(V)
has consequently the dimension
k(n-k)
. Choose again
W\in\Gr_{k}(V)
and a smooth curve
\gamma\colon I\to\Gr_{k}(V)
with
I
an interval containing
0
, and with
\gamma(0)=W
. Choose a complement
W^{c}
for
W
in
V
. Then, possibly after shrinking the interval
I
around
0
, the curve
\gamma
has image in
U_{W,W^{c}}
. It is hence identified via
\Psi_{W,W^{c}}
with a smooth curve
\phi\colon I\to W^{*}\otimes W^{c}
, and its tangent vector at
t=0
is hence

\dot{\phi}(0)\in W^{*}\otimes W^{c}.

Since
W^{c}
is canonically isomorphic to
V/W
, this shows that

T_{W}\Gr_{k}(V)\simeq W^{*}\otimes V/W

via the choice of chart centered at
W
. The following shows that this does not depend on the choice of complement
W^{c}
for
W
. Consider two linear complements
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
for
W
in
V
. Then for each
w_{1}\in W_{1}
there exist
w\in W
and
w_{2}\in W_{2}
such that
w_{1}=w+w_{2}
. Setting
A(w_{1})=w
and
B(w_{1})=w_{2}
defines two linear maps
A=\pr^{W,W_{2}}_{W}\circ\iota_{W_{1}}\colon W_{1}\to W
and
B=\pr^{W,W_{2}}_{W_{2}}\circ\iota_{W_{1}}\colon W_{1}\to W_{2}
. Here,
\iota_{W_{1}}
is the inclusion of
W_{1}
in
V
, and
\pr^{W,W_{2}}_{W}
and
\pr^{W,W_{2}}_{W_{2}}
are the linear projections from
V
on
W
and
W_{2}
defined by the splitting
V=W\oplus W_{2}
. The map
B
is invertible with inverse
\pr^{W,W_{1}}_{W_{1}}\circ\iota_{W_{2}}\colon W_{2}\to W_{1}
. Take
\phi\in W^{*}\otimes W_{1}
. Then for all
w\in W

w+\phi(w)=w+A\phi(w)+B\phi(w)=\underset{\in W}{\underbrace{(\id_{W}+A\phi)(w)}}+\underset{\in W_{2}}{\underbrace{B\phi(w)}}.

If
\grap(\phi)\cap W_{2}=\{0\}
, then the map

\pr^{W,W_{2}}_{W}\arrowvert_{\grap\phi}\colon\grap(\phi)\to W,\qquad w+\phi(w)\to(\id_{W}+A\phi)(w)

is invertible, and so the map

\id_{W}+A\phi=\pr^{W,W_{2}}_{W}\arrowvert_{\grap\phi}\circ I_{\phi}\colon W\to W

is invertible as well,
if
I_{\phi}\colon W\to\grap(\phi)
is the isomorphism
w\mapsto w+\phi(w)
.

It is then easy to see that

\grap(\phi)=\grap\left(B\phi(\id_{W}+A\phi)^{-1}\right),

with the linear map
B\phi(\id_{W}+A\phi)^{-1}\in W^{*}\otimes W_{2}
.

Now take a smooth curve
\gamma\colon I\to\Gr_{k}(V)
with
\gamma(0)=W
. There exists
\epsilon>0
such that

\gamma(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\subseteq\{W^{\prime}\in\Gr_{k}(W)\mid W^{\prime}\oplus W_{1}=V=W^{\prime}\oplus W_{2}\}
. Without loss of generality
I=(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
.
Set
\phi_{1}\colon I\to W^{*}\otimes W_{1}
,

\phi_{1}=\Psi_{W,W_{1}}^{-1}\circ\gamma.

Then by the considerations above the image
\phi_{2}
of
\gamma
in the chart of
\Gr_{k}(V)
defined by
W
and
W_{2}
is given by

\phi_{2}(t)=\Psi_{W,W_{2}}^{-1}\circ\gamma(t)=B\cdot\phi_{1}(t)\cdot(I_{W}+A\phi_{1}(t))^{-1}

for all
t\in I
, and

\begin{split}\dot{\phi}_{2}(0)&=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}\phi_{2}(t)=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}B\cdot\phi_{1}(t)\cdot(I_{W}+A\phi_{1}(t))^{-1}\\
&=B\cdot\dot{\phi}_{1}(0)\cdot(I_{W}+A\phi_{1}(0))^{-1}+B\cdot\phi_{1}(0)\cdot\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}(I_{W}+A\phi_{1}(t))^{-1}\\
&=B\cdot\dot{\phi}_{1}(0)\end{split}

since
\phi_{1}(0)=0
.

By definition of
B
, the canonical projection
\pi_{V/W}\colon V\to V/W
does

\pi_{V/W}(\psi(w))=\pi_{V/W}(A\psi(w)+B\psi(w))=\pi_{V/W}(B\psi(w))

for all
\psi\in W^{*}\otimes W_{1}
and
w\in W
, i.e.

\pi_{V/W}\circ\psi=\pi_{V/W}\circ B\circ\psi.

In particular,

\pi_{V/W}\circ\dot{\phi}_{2}(0)=\pi_{V/W}\circ B\circ\dot{\phi}_{1}(0)=\pi_{V/W}\circ\dot{\phi}_{1}(0)

as elements of
W^{*}\otimes V/W
.

Now consider more generally a curve
\alpha\colon I\to\operatorname{GL}(V)
of isomorphisms of
V
such that
\alpha(0)=\id_{V}
, and take
W\in\Gr_{k}(V)
.
Then
\gamma\colon I\to\Gr_{k}(V)
,
t\mapsto\alpha(t)(W)
is a smooth curve starting at
W
.
Choose as before a complement
W^{c}
to
W
in
V
.
Then the curves

A:=\pr_{W}\circ\alpha\arrowvert_{W}\colon I\to W^{*}\otimes W\quad\text{ and }\quad B:=\pr_{W^{c}}\circ\alpha\arrowvert_{W}\colon I\to W^{*}\otimes W^{c}

are smooth and
A
is invertible on an open neighborhood of
0
in
I
, with
A(0)=\Id_{W}
and
B(0)=0
. Without loss of generality
A
is invertible on
I
.
Then for all
t\in I

\gamma(t)=\alpha_{t}(W)=\{A(t)(w)+B(t)(w)\mid w\in W\}=\{w+B(t)(A(t))^{-1}(w)\mid w\in W\}=\grap(B(t)(A(t))^{-1}).

As an element of
T_{W}\Gr_{k}(W)\simeq W^{*}\otimes V/W
, the vector
\dot{\gamma}(0)
is hence

\begin{split}\dot{\gamma}(0)&=\pi_{V/W}\circ\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}(B(t)(A(t))^{-1})=\pi_{V/W}\circ\left(\dot{B}(0)(A(0))^{-1}-B(0)\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\arrowvert_{t=0}(A(t))^{-1}\right)\\
&=\pi_{V/W}\circ\dot{B}(0)=\pi_{V/W}\circ\dot{\alpha}(0)\arrowvert_{W}.\end{split}

A.2.
The tautological bundle and several vector bundles constructed from it

The tautological bundle
\pi\colon\Taut_{k}(V)\to\Gr_{k}(V)
over
\Gr_{k}(V)
is defined fibrewise by

\Taut_{k}(V)\arrowvert_{W}\coloneqq\{W\}\times W\subset\Gr_{k}(V)\times V,

and it is a smooth vector subbundle of the product vector bundle
\Gr_{k}(V)\times V
. That is, the vector space structure on
E_{W}
is given by:
(W,w)+(W,w^{\prime})=(W,w+w^{\prime})
, while the projection
\pi\colon\Taut_{k}(V)\to\Gr_{k}(V)
is given by
\pi(W,w)=W
. Let
U:=U_{W,W^{c}}\simeq W^{*}\otimes W^{c}
be a neighborhood around
W\in\Gr_{k}(V)
defined as above by a choice of complement
W^{c}
for
W
in
V
.
Then

\pi^{-1}(U)\to U\times W,\quad(\grap(\phi),v)\mapsto(\phi,\pr_{W}(v))

for all
\phi\in W^{*}\otimes W^{c}
and all
v\in\grap(\phi)
,
is a smooth trivialisation of
\Taut_{k}(V)
around
W
, with smooth inverse

U\times W\to\pi^{-1}(U),\quad(\phi,w)\mapsto(\grap(\phi),w+\phi(w)).

The smooth annihilator

\Taut_{k}^{\circ}(V):=\{(W,l)\in\Gr_{k}(V)\times V^{*}\mid l(w)=0\text{ for all }w\in W\}

of
\Taut_{k}(V)
as a subbundle of
\Gr_{k}(V)\times V
is denoted by
\pi_{\Taut^{\circ}}\colon\Taut_{k}^{\circ}(V)\to\Gr_{k}(V)
. It is a smooth subbundle of
\Gr_{k}(V)\times V^{*}
because a smooth chart

U
of
\Gr_{k}(V)
as above trivialises
\Taut_{k}^{\circ}(V)
via the map

\pi_{\Taut^{\circ}}^{-1}(U)\to U\times W^{\circ},\quad(\grap(\phi),l)\mapsto(\phi,l\arrowvert_{W^{c}})

with smooth inverse

U\times W^{\circ}\to\pi_{\Taut^{\circ}}^{-1}(U),\quad(\phi,l)\mapsto(\grap(\phi),l-\phi^{*}l).

Here, the canonical identifications
W^{\circ}=(W^{c})^{*}
and
(W^{c})^{\circ}=W^{*}
are used.

The dual vector bundle
\pi_{\Taut^{*}}\colon\Taut_{k}^{*}(V)\to\Gr_{k}(V)
is then as usual canonically isomorphic to the quotient

\Taut_{k}^{*}(V)\simeq\frac{\Gr_{k}(V)\times V^{*}}{\Taut_{k}^{\circ}(V)}\to\Gr_{k}(V).

Again, the smooth chart
U
of
\Gr_{k}(V)
around
W
trivialises
\Taut_{k}^{*}(V)
via

\pi_{\Taut^{*}}^{-1}(U)\simeq\frac{U\times V^{*}}{\pi_{\Taut^{\circ}}^{-1}(U)}\longrightarrow U\times W^{*}\simeq U\times(W^{c})^{\circ},

(\grap(\phi),l+\grap(\phi)^{\circ})\mapsto(\phi,l\arrowvert_{W}+l\arrowvert_{W^{c}}\circ\phi)

with the smooth inverse

(\grap(\phi),l)\mapsto(\grap(\phi),l+\grap(\phi)^{\circ}).

Finally, the quotient vector bundle

\overline{\pi}\colon\frac{\Gr_{k}(V)\times V}{\Taut_{k}(V)}\to\Gr_{k}(V)

is locally trivialised by the smooth map

\overline{\pi}^{-1}(U)\to U\times W^{c},\quad(\grap(\phi),x+\grap(\phi))\mapsto(\phi,\pr_{W^{c}}(x)-\phi(\pr_{W}(x)))

with the smooth inverse

U\times W^{c}\to\overline{\pi}^{-1}(U),\quad(\phi,x)\mapsto(\grap(\phi),x+\grap(\phi)).

The vector bundle

E:=(\Gr_{k}(V)\times V^{*})\otimes(\operatorname{Taut}_{k}(V))^{*}\otimes\frac{\Gr_{k}(V)\times V}{\operatorname{Taut}_{k}(V)}\longrightarrow\Gr_{k}(V)

comes with the smooth vector bundle projection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gr_{k}(V)\times(V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gr_{k}(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E}}

sending
(W,\phi)
to

(W,\pi_{V/W}\circ\phi\arrowvert_{V\otimes W}).

E
is trivialised over the open subset
U=U_{W,W^{c}}
of
\Gr_{k}(V)
by

\Phi\colon U\times(V^{*}\otimes W^{*}\otimes V/W)\to E\arrowvert_{U},

(\grap(\phi),\theta\otimes(\eta\arrowvert_{W}+\eta\arrowvert_{W^{c}}\circ\phi)\otimes(\pr_{W^{c}}(x)-\phi(\pr_{W}(x))))\,\mapsto\,(\phi,\theta\otimes(\eta+\grap(\phi)^{\circ})\otimes(x+\grap(\phi))).

Conversely, the inverse of this smooth vector bundle isomorphism sends
(\grap(\phi),\omega)
with
\omega\in V^{*}\otimes\grap(\phi)^{*}\otimes V/\grap(\phi)
to
(\phi,\tilde{\omega})
with
\tilde{\omega}\in V^{*}\otimes W^{*}\otimes V/W
defined by

(52)

\tilde{\omega}(v,w)=\pr_{W^{c}}(\omega(v,w+\phi(w)))-\phi(\pr_{W}(\omega(v,w+\phi(w))))

for all
v\in V
and
w\in W
.
Note that the isomorphism
\Phi\colon U\times(V^{*}\otimes W^{*}\otimes V/W)\to E\arrowvert_{U}
is the identity on
E\arrowvert_{W}=\{W\}\times(V^{*}\otimes W^{*}\otimes V/W)
.

A choice of inner product
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle
on
V
defines a map

\operatorname{orth}\colon\Gr_{k}(V)\to\Gr_{n-k}(V)
(where
n=\dim V
) sending a
k
-dimensional subspace of
V
to its orthogonal complement.
For
\phi\in W^{*}\otimes\operatorname{orth}(W)
, the adjoint map
\phi^{t}\in(\operatorname{orth}(W))^{*}\otimes W
with respect to
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle
is defined as usual by

\langle\phi(w),u\rangle=\langle w,\phi^{t}(u)\rangle

for all
w\in W
and
u\in\operatorname{orth}(W)
. It is then easy to see that in the coordinates on
\Gr_{k}(V)
and
\Gr_{n-k}(V)
defined by the splitting
W\oplus\operatorname{orth}(W)
, the map
\operatorname{orth}
sends
\phi\in W^{*}\otimes\operatorname{orth}(W)
to
-\phi^{t}\in(\operatorname{orth}(W))^{*}\otimes W
. Hence, it is a smooth map.

It defines the vector bundle morphism

s\colon\frac{\Gr_{k}(V)\times V}{\Taut_{k}(V)}\to\Gr_{k}(V)\times V,\qquad(W,x+W)\mapsto(W,\underset{=:s_{W}(x+W)}{\underbrace{\pr_{\operatorname{orth}(W)}(x)}}),

where for each
W\in\Gr_{k}(V)
the map
\pr_{\operatorname{orth}(W)}\colon V\to V
is the projection of
V
on
\operatorname{orth}(W)
defined by the splitting
V=W\oplus\operatorname{orth}(W)
of
V
.
The map
s
is smooth since

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gr_{k}(V)\times V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gr_{k}(V)\times V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(W,v)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(W,\pr_{\operatorname{orth}(W)}(v))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\frac{\Gr_{k}(V)\times V}{\Taut_{k}(V)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(W,v+W)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

commutes and the top map
\tilde{s}
is clearly a smooth vector bundle morphism, while the left projection is a fibration of smooth vector bundles (hence a smooth surjective submersion).
The vector bundle morphism
s
splits the short exact sequence of vector bundles

0\longrightarrow\Taut_{k}(V)\longrightarrow\Gr_{k}(V)\times V\longrightarrow\frac{\Gr_{k}(V)\times V}{\Taut_{k}(V)}\longrightarrow 0

over the identity on
\Gr_{k}(V)
. Similarly,
the smooth vector bundle morphism

\pr\colon\Gr_{k}(V)\times V\longrightarrow\Taut_{k}(V),\qquad(W,x)\to(W,\pr_{W}(x))

is defined by the projections

\pr_{W}\colon V\to W
of
V
on
W
defined by the same splittings
V=W\oplus\operatorname{orth}(W)
of
V
.
The vector bundle morphisms
s
and
\pr
then define together the smooth splitting

S\colon E\to\Gr_{k}(V)\times(V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V)

of
P\colon\Gr_{k}(V)\times(V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V)\to E

by

S\colon(W,\eta)\mapsto(W,\underset{=:S_{W}(\eta)\in V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V}{\underbrace{s_{W}\circ\eta\circ(\id_{V}\otimes\pr_{W})}}).
